Non-equilibrium fluctuations for the stirring process with births and deaths
P. Birmpa, P. Gonçalves, D. Tsagkarogiannis

Abstract
We consider the one-dimensional stirring process on the segment { − N , … , N } \{-N,\ldots,N\} , coupled to boundary dynamics that inject particles from the right reservoir and remove particles from the left reservoir, each acting on a window of size K K . We investigate the non-equilibrium fluctuations of the system, starting from a product measure associated with a smooth initial profile. Given our initial state, the fluctuations are given by an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process whose characteristic operators are the Laplacian and gradient operators. The domains of these operators include functions with boundary conditions that depend on the hydrodynamic profile. A central ingredient in our analysis is the derivation of sharp bounds on the space and space–time v v -functions of arbitrary degree for the centered occupation variables. In particular, we prove that the v v -functions of degree 2 2 and 3 3 are of order N − 1 N^{-1} , while those of degree at least 4 4 are of order N − 1 − � N^{-1-\zeta} for some � > 0 \zeta>0 .



1
Introduction

The derivation of macroscopic equations of classical fluid mechanics from a large-scale description of conserved quantities in Newtonian particle systems is a long-standing problem in mathematical physics. In order to simplify this
challenge, a common approach is to consider random models from statistical mechanics, such as interacting
particle systems (IPS). In the spirit of Boltzmann’s program, one first identifies the stationary states of the system, then characterizes them through the relevant
thermodynamic quantities, and only afterwards analyzes the behaviour of the system out of equilibrium.

IPS were introduced into the mathematics community by
Spitzer
[
20
]
, although they had already been widely used in physics and
biophysics as microscopic stochastic models whose dynamics conserve certain quantities.
The basic assumption is that each particle behaves as a continuous-time random walk on a discretization of the macroscopic space. This viewpoint enables a probabilistic analysis of the underlying discrete system. For a detailed and formal definition of IPS, we refer the reader to
[
17
]
. The evolution of the particles is Markovian: the future dynamics, conditioned on the past, depends only on the current configuration. The macroscopic space is discretized using a scaling parameter
0<\epsilon:=N^{-1}
, which will eventually be sent to zero to connect microscopic and macroscopic descriptions. Each site of the discrete lattice can host a certain number of particles, and these particles evolve according to local, prescribed rules that determine the random dynamics of the system.

One of the most studied IPS is the exclusion process, a microscopic random system in which each site can be occupied by at most one particle. Its dynamics can be described as follows. On each bond of the discrete space, say
\{x,x+1\}
, we attach an independent Poisson process with intensity
N^{2}
. Whenever a mark occurs on the Poisson process associated with the bond
\{x,x+1\}
, the particles located at
x
and
x+1
attempt to exchange their positions according to a prescribed transition probability
p(x,y)
. The exclusion rule enforces that jumps resulting in two particles occupying the same site are suppressed.

The model under investigation.
In this article we focus on the exclusion process also called stirring process and we consider
p(x,x+1)=p(x+1,x)=N^{2}
, so that the system is symmetric. Under this dynamics, the particles move in the system, by exchanging their positions with their neighbors, after a mark of the Poisson process. We note that the number of particles is conserved under this dynamics. We supplement this exchange dynamics with a Glauber dynamics that injects and removes particles in the system. The choice of the macroscopic space in this article is the one dimensional interval
[-1,1]
and the corresponding microscopic
space is the space
\{-N,-N+1,\dots,N-1,N\}
. The Glauber dynamics acts on two possible set of sites : those at distance less than or equal
K\geq 2
to the points
-N,N
. The injection and removal rates are defined in the following way: from the right of the system a particle can be injected in the system at a site
y
in
{N-K+1,\dots,N}
if the site
y
is the first empty site on the window
{N-K,\dots,N}
counting it from right to left. A particle in the left window of size
K
, namely in the set of sites
\{-N,\dots,-N+K-1\}
can be removed from the site
z\in\{-N,\dots,-N+K-1\}
if
z
is the first occupied site in that window counting it from left to right. Note that the superposition of this dynamics with the exchange dynamics described previously, destroys the conservation law of the system, though the number of particles is the quantity that we want to analyze.
We also note that this dynamics was first introduced in
[
4
]
but the boundary dynamics was slowed down with respect to the exchange dynamics by a factor of
\epsilon
and later the same dynamics slowed by a factor
\epsilon^{\theta}
, with
\theta>0

was also explored in
[
7
,
8
]
.

The hydrodynamic limit.

The first challenge concerns the description of the space-time evolution of the number of particles in the system. To address this, we consider the empirical measure of the density defined in (
2.7
), which consists of a random measure that associates the weight
\epsilon
to every occupied site of the system and zero to the others. The goal is to deduce the partial differential equation (PDE),
the hydrodynamic equation
, that governs the space-time evolution of the density of particles, from the underlying random dynamics.
One way to guess for the possible nature of the PDE is to consider the discrete profile, that accounts for the average of the occupation variables in the system. If we denote by
\eta_{t}(x)
the quantity of particles at site
x
and time
t\epsilon^{-2}
and if the system starts from a measure
\mu^{\epsilon}
we denote
the discrete profile by
\rho^{\epsilon}_{t}(x)=\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[\eta_{t}(x)]
. From Kolmogorov’s forward equation, we get
\partial_{t}\rho^{\epsilon}_{t}(x)=\mdmathbb E_{\mu^{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}[L_{\epsilon}(\eta_{t}(x))]
, where
L_{\epsilon}
is the generator of our Markov process.
We observe that the boundary dynamics defined above increases the degree of polynomials in
\eta
at the points in the boundary windows. The reason is that the Glauber dynamics injects and removes particles at a site
y
with a rate that depends on the configuration
\eta
at the points between the right or left boundary reservoir and
y
. Therefore, the previous discrete PDE for points in the boundary window involves the expectation of terms given by products of
\eta
’s. This is the source of the difficulty when dealing with this model. In order to close the previous equation in terms of
\rho_{t}^{\epsilon}(x)
we have to consider an auxiliary function that solves the same evolution equation as
\rho_{t}^{\epsilon}(x)
but at the points in the boundary window we assume that the variables involved in the product are uncorrelated. This simple observation allows factorizing the expectation of those products into products of the corresponding
\rho
’s. As a consequence we obtain the exact PDE solved by the auxiliary function that we denote by
\rho_{\epsilon}(x,t)
. The advantage in considering the latter function
\rho_{\epsilon}(x,t)
with respect to
\rho_{t}^{\epsilon}(x)
is that the former solves a closed evolution equation while the latter depends on higher-order
moments.

In
[
4
]
the authors assumed that the function
\rho_{\epsilon}(\cdot,0)
converges weakly as
\epsilon\to 0
to a macroscopic profile
u_{0}(\cdot)\in L^{\infty}([-1,1],[0,1])
and deduced the hydrodynamic limit by showing that
\rho_{\epsilon}(x,t)
and
\rho_{t}
are close in the sup norm, where the function
\rho_{t}
is the solution of the heat equation with some boundary conditions, see Theorem

2.6
for a precise statement. We note that some years later, the same model but taking the boundary dynamics slowed down with respect to the exchange dynamics with a factor
\epsilon^{\theta}
, with
\theta>0
was studied in
[
7
,
8
]
. There, the authors derived the hydrodynamic limit when
\theta\geq 1
using the entropy method developed by
[
11
]
and when
\theta<1
using the method of propagation of chaos, the same method used by
[
4
]
to establish the result in the case
\theta=1
.
In the same article, the authors were also able to show that
\rho_{\epsilon}(x,t)
and
\rho_{t}^{\epsilon}(x)
are close with respect to the sup-norm. This last result was obtained as a consequence of a much stronger result which was related to correlation estimates, which we now explain in detail.

The
v
-function.

Given an initial measure
\mu^{\epsilon}
, the correlation of particles at sites
x,y
is defined as the expectation with respect to that given measure of the centered variables
\eta_{t}(x)-\rho_{t}^{\epsilon}(x)
and
\eta_{t}(y)-\rho_{t}^{\epsilon}(y)
. As mentioned above, the evolution equation for
\rho_{t}^{\epsilon}
involves higher–order moments, which makes it more convenient to consider instead the correlations of the centered variables
\eta_{t}(x)-\rho_{\epsilon}(x,t)
and
\eta_{t}(y)-\rho_{\epsilon}(y,t)
. These correlations are referred to as the
v
-functions.
The definition above concerns only two spatial variables, but it extends in a straightforward manner to any number of variables. For that reason, the
v
-functions will be denoted precisely by
v^{\epsilon}_{n}(\underline{x},t)
, where
n
represents the number of spatial variables and the cardinality of
\underline{x}
is
n
. From Kolmogorov’s identity one can get an evolution equation for the
v
-functions
which allows to apply Duhamel’s formula and give a probabilistic interpretation of the solution in terms of the stirring process. As the
Markov generator increases the degree of the
v
-function, the probabilistic interpretation uses a branching process that keeps track of the action of the Glauber dynamics, in the sense that it takes into account how many particles are created/destroyed at the boundary windows.
The estimate for the
v
-functions obtained in
[
6
]
, following the strategy developed in
[
18
]
, keeps track of the number
n
of spatial variables and is different depending on whether we consider short or long times.

We recall this estimate here: for a fixed integer number
K
such that
K\geq 2
as given in Section
2.1
.

{|v_{n}^{\epsilon}(\underline{x},t)|}\leq\begin{cases}c_{n}(\epsilon^{-2}t)^{-c^{*}n},&\text{for }t\leq\epsilon^{\beta^{*}}\\
c_{n}\epsilon^{(2-\beta^{*})c^{*}n},&\text{for }\epsilon^{\beta^{*}}\leq t\leq\tau\log\epsilon^{-1}\end{cases}

where
\beta^{*}<2
,
\tau>0
and
c^{*}<\frac{1}{4(K+2)}
. We observe that when
n
is sufficiently large and
\epsilon<1
, the estimate can become of order
\epsilon
or even smaller. However, for small
n
, the estimate is not so good since the exponent of
\epsilon
,
c^{*}n
is small.
In this work, whose purpose is to derive the equation for the fluctuation field (which scales with
\sqrt{\epsilon}
rather that
\epsilon
) we need to obtain better estimates for the case of small
n
, giving up the more delicate
n
-dependent estimate. However, the latter will still be used. Hence, we show that for
n=1,2
the bound is very close to
\epsilon
, for
3\leq n\leq K
it is exactly of order
\epsilon
, and for
n\geq K+1
it is of order
\epsilon^{1+\zeta}
with
\zeta>0
for times
0<t\leq T
, for some
T>0
. Furthermore, we provide a bound which holds for a fixed time horizon
T
in contrast to the aforementioned result in
[
6
]
. The extension to times
T\log\epsilon^{-1}
would require more work.
Our approach proceeds by deriving the discrete evolution equation for
v_{n}(\cdot,t)
and obtaining its integral form, which contains all possible scenarios of how particles move in the system. The proof then relies on a direct and detailed estimation of these scenarios, primarily using Gaussian kernel bounds. The time integration in the integral form of the discrete equation for
v
-functions, poses additional challenges, which we overcome through several techniques.

The space-time
v
-function.
Given an initial measure
\mu^{\epsilon}
, the one–point space–time correlation of
particles at sites
y
and
x
involves two times
s<r
, respectively: particles are located
at site
y
at time
s
, and at site
x
at time
r
. It is defined as the
expectation, with respect to
\mu^{\epsilon}
, of the centered occupation variables

\eta_{s}(y)-\rho_{\epsilon}(s,y)
and

\eta_{r}(x)-\rho_{\epsilon}(r,x)
. This definition
extends in a straightforward way to any number of spatial variables at the two
times
s
and
r
.

As in the case of the space
v
-functions, we derive a discrete evolution
equation for the space–time
v
-functions by fixing the locations of the
particles at time
s
and analyzing the evolution of the positions at the
later time
r
. The integral form of the discrete evolution equation includes all possible scenarios of particle’s motion within the system between
times
s
and
r
. In contrast to the purely spatial case, the integral
form of the discrete equation now contains an initial term at time
s
, whose estimation
requires bounding the expectation, with respect to the stirring process, of
the
v
-function at time
s
evaluated at the (random) configuration occupied at
that time. Controlling this term introduces additional technical difficulties. We show that this expectation is of order
\epsilon
for
n=2,3
, and of order

\epsilon^{1+\zeta}
for
n\geq 4
for some
\zeta>0
. This yields an additional
improvement of the overall estimates and, in turn, strengthens the final bounds
on the space–time
v
-functions.

Non-equilibrium fluctuations.

Our goal in this article is to understand the behavior of fluctuations around the hydrodynamical profile
\rho_{t}
, solution of the hydrodynamic equation given in (
2.5
). To that end we consider the density fluctuation field which is nothing but the integral of a test function
H
with respect to the empirical measure properly centered and rescaled, see (
2.19
) for the exact definition. We show that when the system starts from a product measure whose marginals are given in terms of a smooth profile, see
Assumption 1
, the sequence of density fields converges to the solution of an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process given in (
2.23
). This choice of initial condition is essential because it allows us to invoke the estimates from
[
6
]
, which are applicable only when the system starts from a product measure. If we had similar apriori estimates for the space of
v
-functions starting from general initial conditions, our results could also be less restrictive.
The strategy of the proof is based on associating the Dynkin’s martingales to the density fluctuation fields. Those will be close to the martingales appearing in the definition of the corresponding Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process, see (
2.24
). Showing that these martingales are close is one of the biggest challenges of this work and it is based on refined bounds on space
v
-functions but also on space-time
v
-functions, as we have explained before, together with a proper choice of the space of test functions, that we explain now.

The choice of the space of test functions.
Our density fluctuation field acts on functions that are
C^{1}
in time and
C^{2}
in space. The regularity in time is needed so that we can apply Dynkin’s formula to obtain a sequence of martingales associated to the density field and the regularity in space is needed since the hydrodynamic equation is the heat equation and we expect the limit field to satisfy the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck equation whose characteristic operators are the Laplacian and the gradient. Moreover, due to the action of the boundary dynamics on the conserved quantity, we obtain a contribution given in terms of products of occupation variables, see the last two lines of (
3.6
). The exchange dynamics also contributes with boundary terms at the end points
N
and
-N
, and those correspond to the second line of (
3.6
). After centering these terms coming from the two contributions, we split them into degree one and higher-order degree. The degree one terms are shown not to contribute in the limit under a specific choice of the boundary conditions for the test functions, see (
3.16
). The remaining terms, which have a higher order degree as functions of
\eta
, are shown not to contribute in the limit as a consequence of our refined estimates on the space and space-time
v
-functions.

Future problems.
We conclude by collecting a few interesting problems, closely related to the ones investigated
in this article, that remain open for further study.
A first natural question concerns the derivation of the non-equilibrium fluctuations for more general initial conditions as, for example, the non-equilibrium stationary state (NESS). It is easy to check that the NESS is not of product form, and given the assumptions that we impose on our initial measure our result does not include the fluctuations from the NESS. A second direction is to analyze our results in the case the system is put in contact with slow/fast boundary dynamics and to explore what is the dependence on the strength of the dynamics in the
v
-functions estimates. A third line of investigation concerns the extension of our methods to other boundary
mechanisms. For instance, it would be interesting to treat boundary dynamics that drive the system to the heat equation with non-linear Robin boundary conditions, such as in the model recently studied in
[
15
]
.

2
Statement of results

2.1
The model

Let
N\in\mdmathbb{N}
represent the scaling parameter, which we will consider in the limit
N\to\infty
. Fix an arbitrary integer
1\leq K\leq N
. We examine the stirring process on the discrete subspace
\Lambda_{N}:=[-N,N]\subset\mdmathbb{Z}
. Particle configurations are represented by elements
\eta\in{}_{N}:=\{0,1\}^{\Lambda_{N}}
, where
\eta(x)=1
indicates the presence of a particle at site
x
, and
\eta(x)=0
otherwise. The stirring process, incorporating births and deaths, is a Markov process
\{\eta_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
fully characterized by its infinitesimal generator given by

L_{\epsilon}:=\epsilon^{-2}\big(L_{0}+\epsilon L_{b,-}+\epsilon L_{b,+}\big),\quad\epsilon\equiv\frac{1}{N},

(2.1)

where
L_{0}
is the generator of the stirring process in
\Lambda_{N}
, namely

L_{0}f(\eta):=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x=-N}^{N-1}[f(\eta^{(x,x+1)})-f(\eta)]

(2.2)

and

L_{b,\pm}f(\eta):=\frac{j}{2}\sum_{x\in I_{\pm}}D_{\pm}\eta(x)[f(\eta^{(x)})-f(\eta)\Big],

(2.3)

with
j>0
,
I_{+}=[N-K+1,N]
,
I_{-}=[-N,-N+K-1]
,

\begin{split}&D_{+}u(x)=[1-u(x)]u(x+1)u(x+2)\cdots u(N),\;x\in I_{+},\\
&D_{-}u(x)=u(x)[1-u(x-1)][1-u(x-2)]\cdots[1-u(-N)],\;x\in I_{-}\,.\end{split}

(2.4)

Note that the generator includes a factor of
\epsilon^{-2}
, indicating that the process is considered on the diffusive time scale. In (
2.3
),
\eta^{x,x+1}
, with
-N\leq x\leq N-1
, represents the configuration obtained from
\eta
by exchanging the occupation variables
\eta(x)
and
\eta(x+1)
:

\eta^{(x,x+1)}(y)=\begin{cases}\eta(x+1),&y=x\;,\\
\eta(x),&y=x+1\;,\\
\eta(y),&y\neq x,x+1\;,\\
\end{cases}

while
\eta^{(x)}
, for
x\in I_{\pm}
, is the configuration obtained by flipping the
occupation variable
\eta(x)
:

\eta^{(x)}(y)=\begin{cases}\eta(x),&y\neq x\;,\\
1-\eta(y),&y=x\,.\\
\end{cases}

The operator
N^{2}L_{0}
is the generator of the stirring process, where neighboring particles exchange positions at rate
N^{2}/2
. The operator
NL_{b,\pm}
represents the generator of a birth (resp. death) process: at rate
jN/2
, a particle is created (resp. destroyed) at the first empty site starting from
N
(resp.
-N
) in
I_{+}
(resp.
I_{-}
). If no site is empty (resp. occupied), the birth (resp. death) process is aborted, see the figure below.

-N
\cdots
x-1
x
N
N-1
N-2
\tfrac{jN}{2}
\tfrac{jN}{2}
\times
\tfrac{N^{2}}{2}

2.2
Hydrodynamic limit

In this section, we review the hydrodynamic limit of our model, as developed in
[
4
,
7
]
. The corresponding hydrodynamic equation is the linear heat equation on the “macroscopic” interval
(-1,1)
, subject to Robin boundary conditions at
\pm 1
. Before presenting the result, we introduce several key definitions. The hydrodynamic limit will also be used in deriving various estimates needed to establish the non-equilibrium fluctuations of the model.

We denote by
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}}
the
\mdmathbb L^{2}
-norm with respect to the Lebesgue measure in
[-1,1]
and by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
the corresponding inner-product. For some fixed time horizon
T>0
, we denote by
C^{m,n}([0,T]\times[-1,1])
the set of functions defined on
[0,T]\times[-1,1]
that are
m
times differentiable on the first variable and
n
times differentiable on the second variable, with continuous derivatives. Now we define the Sobolev space
\mathcal{H}^{1}
on
[-1,1]
. For that purpose, we define the semi inner-product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{1}
on the set
C^{\infty}([-1,1])
by
\langle G,H\rangle_{1}=\langle\partial_{u}G\,,\partial_{u}H\rangle

and the corresponding semi-norm is denoted by
\|\cdot\|_{1}
.

Definition 2.1
.

The Sobolev space
\mathcal{H}^{1}
on
[-1,1]
is the Hilbert space defined as the completion of
C^{\infty}([-1,1])
for the norm

\|\cdot\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}^{2}:=\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\cdot\|^{2}_{1}.

Its elements elements coincide a.e. with continuous functions. The space
L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{1})
is the set of measurable functions
f:[0,T]\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{1}
such that

\int^{T}_{0}\|f_{s}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}ds<\infty.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
\rho_{0}:[-1,1]\rightarrow[0,1]
be a measurable function. We say that
\rho:[0,T]\times[-1,1]\to[-1,1]
is a weak solution of the heat equation with Robin boundary conditions

\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}\rho(u,t)=\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}_{u}\,{\rho}(u,t),\quad(t,u)\in[0,T]\times(0,1),\\
&\partial_{u}\rho(t,-1)=\widetilde{D}_{-}(\rho(-1,t)),\quad t\in(0,T]\\
&\partial_{u}\rho(t,1)=\widetilde{D}_{+}(\rho(1,t)),\quad t\in(0,T]\\
&\rho(0,\cdot)=\rho_{0}(\cdot),\end{cases}

(2.5)

where

\widetilde{D}_{-}(\rho)=j(1-(1-\rho)^{K})\quad\textrm{and}\quad\widetilde{D}_{+}(\rho)=j(1-\rho^{K})

(2.6)

if the following two conditions hold:

1.

\rho\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{1})
,

2.

\rho
satisfies the weak formulation:

\begin{split}&\langle\rho_{t},f_{t}\rangle-\langle\rho_{0},f_{0}\rangle-\int_{0}^{t}\langle\rho_{s},\Big(\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}_{u}+\partial_{s}\Big)f_{s}\,\rangle{ds}+\frac{1}{2}\int^{t}_{0}\rho_{s}(1)\partial_{u}f_{s}(1)-\rho_{s}(-1)\partial_{u}f_{s}(-1)\,ds\\
&-\int^{t}_{0}f_{s}(1)\widetilde{D}_{+}(\rho(1,s))ds+\int_{0}^{t}f_{s}(-1)\widetilde{D}_{-}(\rho(-1,s))ds=0,\end{split}

for all
t\in[0,T]
, any function
f\in C^{1,2}([0,T]\times[-1,1])
.

Remark 2.3
.

We warn the reader not to confuse the functions
\widetilde{D}_{\pm}
defined just above with the operators defined in (
2.4
).

Remark 2.4
.

We observe that the weak solution of the PDE given above is unique. We refer the reader to the proof in
[
7
]
.

Observe that the above equation coincides with the case
K=1
of
[
9
]
by taking there
\alpha=0
and
\beta=1
and
j=1
and in that case the Robin boundary conditions are linear.

Now we state the hydrodynamic limit of the process
\{\eta_{{t}}\}_{t\geq{0}}
. For that purpose, let
{\mathcal{M}}^{+}
be the space of positive measures on
[-1,1]
with total mass bounded by
1
equipped with the weak topology. For any configuration
\eta\in{}_{N}
we define the empirical measure
{\pi^{N}(\eta,\cdot)\in{\mathcal{M}}^{+}}
on
[-1,1]
as

\pi^{\epsilon}(\eta,du)=\epsilon\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}\eta(x)\delta_{\epsilon x}\left(du\right),

(2.7)

where
\delta_{a}
is a Dirac mass on
a\in[-1,1]
. Given the trajectory
\{\eta_{{t}}\}_{t\geq{0}}
of the
accelerated
process, we further introduce

\pi^{\epsilon}_{t}(\eta,du):=\pi^{\epsilon}(\eta_{t},du)
the empirical measure at the macroscopic time
t
.
We denote by
\mdmathbb P_{\mu^{\epsilon}}
the probability measure in the Skorohod space
\mathcal{D}([0,T],{}_{N})
induced by the Markov process
\{\eta_{{{t}}}\}_{t\geq{0}}
and the initial probability measure
\mu^{\epsilon}
and
\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}
denotes the expectation w.r.t.
\mdmathbb P_{\mu^{\epsilon}}
.

Theorem 2.5
.

Let
\rho_{0}:[-1,1]\rightarrow[0,1]
be a measurable function and let
\{\mu^{\epsilon}\}_{\epsilon}
be a sequence of probability measures in
N
such that for any continuous function
f:[-1,1]\rightarrow\mdmathbb{R}
and every
\delta>0

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mu^{\epsilon}\Big(\eta\in{}_{N}:\Big|\int_{-1}^{1}f(u){\pi^{\epsilon}}(\eta,du)-\int_{-1}^{1}f(u)\rho_{0}(u)du\Big|>\delta\Big)=0.

(2.8)

Then, for any
t\in[0,T]
,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mdmathbb P_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\Big(\Big|\int_{-1}^{1}f(u)\pi_{t}^{\epsilon}(\eta,du)-\int_{-1}^{1}f(u)\rho(u,t)du\Big|>\delta\Big)=0,

where
\rho(\cdot,t)
is the unique weak solution of (
2.5
).

The next theorem, derived in
[
4
]
, also provides the hydrodynamic limit for the stirring process with births and deaths. It is analogous to the previous one, but written in a different fashion. We decided to include both since some of these results will be needed in our proofs. For
x\in\Lambda_{N}
and
t\geq 0
, let us denote

\rho^{\epsilon}_{t}(x)=\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[\eta_{t}(x)].

(2.9)

We observe that the function
\rho^{\epsilon}_{t}(x)
, from Kolmogorov’s forward equation, satisfies an evolution equation given by
\partial_{t}\rho^{\epsilon}_{t}(x)=\mdmathbb E_{\mu^{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}[L_{\epsilon}(\eta_{t}(x))]
, see (
A.1
). Since the boundary generators increase the degree of polynomial functions of
\eta
we do not get a closed equation for
\partial_{t}\rho^{\epsilon}_{t}(x)
. Therefore we introduce the function
\rho_{\epsilon}(x,t)
as the
solution of

\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}\rho_{\epsilon}(x,t)=\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{\epsilon}\rho_{\epsilon}(x,t)+\epsilon^{-1}\frac{j}{2}\Big(\mathbf{1}_{x\in I_{+}}D_{+}\rho_{\epsilon}(x,t)-\mathbf{1}_{x\in I_{-}}D_{-}\rho_{\epsilon}(x,t)\Big)\\
&{\rho_{\epsilon}(x,0)=u_{0}(\epsilon x)},\end{cases}

(2.10)

where
\Delta_{\epsilon}=\epsilon^{-2}\Delta
,
\Delta

the discrete Laplacian in
\Lambda_{N}
with reflecting boundary conditions:

\displaystyle\Delta u(x)=u(x+1)+u(x-1)-2u(x),\qquad|x|<\epsilon^{-1}

(2.11)

\displaystyle\Delta u(\pm N)=u(\pm(N-1),t)-u(\pm N,t).

(2.12)

Theorem 2.6
.

[
4
, Theorem 1]

Suppose that the initial datum
\rho_{\epsilon}(\cdot,0)
defined on
\Lambda_{N}
, and taking values in
[0,1]
converges weakly as
\epsilon\to 0
to
u_{0}(\cdot)\in L^{\infty}([-1,1],[0,1])
in the sense

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}\rho_{\epsilon}(\cdot,0)\phi(\epsilon x)=\int_{[-1,1]}u_{0}(r)\phi(r)dr,\quad\text{for every}\;\phi\in L^{\infty}([-1,1],\mdmathbb{R}).

(2.13)

Then, there is
\rho(x,t)
,
x\in[-1,1]
,
t>0
so that for any
t_{1}>t_{0}>0

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\sup_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}\sup_{t_{0}\leq t\leq t_{1}}|\rho_{\epsilon}(x,t)-\rho(\epsilon x,t)|=0,

(2.14)

where the function
\rho(x,t)
is the unique solution of the integral equation

\displaystyle\rho(x,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{[-1,1]}P_{t}(x,r^{\prime})u_{0}(r^{\prime})dr^{\prime}+\frac{j}{2}\int_{0}^{t}P_{s}(x,1)(1-\rho(x,t-s)^{K})

(2.15)

\displaystyle-P_{s}(x,-1)(1-(1-\rho(-1,t-s))^{K})ds

and
P_{t}(x,r^{\prime})
is the density kernel of the semigroup (also denoted as
P_{t}
) with generator

\Delta/2
,
\Delta
the Laplacian in
[-1,1]
with reflecting, Neumann, boundary conditions.

Remark 2.7
.

We note that the solution
\rho(x,t)
of the previous equation is smooth. This has been observed in Remark 2.4 of
[
4
]
.

Our goal in this article is to understand how is the behavior of the fluctuations around the hydrodynamical profile.

2.3
Fluctuations

In this subsection, we present our results regarding the non-equilibrium fluctuation of the stirring process with births and deaths, and the convergence of the density fluctuation field given in Definition
2.10
to a mean zero generalized Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process. These results are detailed in Theorem
2.14
. To begin, we recall from
[
2
]
the definition of the space of test functions on which the functional associated with the system’s density fluctuations acts, as well as the semigroup associated with the hydrodynamic limit.

2.3.1
The space of test functions

Definition 2.8
.

Fix a solution of the hydrodynamic equation
\rho(u,t)
(which by Remark
2.7
is a smooth function) and let
\mdmathbb S
denote the following subspace of functions in
C^{1,2}([0,T]\times[-1,1])
that satisfy for all
t\in(0,T]

\begin{split}&\partial_{u}H_{t}(-1)=\widetilde{D}^{\prime}_{-}(\rho(-1,t))H_{t}(-1),\\
&\partial_{u}H_{t}(1)=\widetilde{D}_{+}^{\prime}(\rho(1,t))H_{t}(1),\end{split}

(2.16)

where
\widetilde{D}_{\pm}
were defined in (
2.6
).

Definition 2.9
.

Let
\mdmathbb S^{\prime}
be the topological dual of
\mdmathbb S
with respect to the topology generated by the seminorms

\|f\|_{k}=\sup_{u\in[-1,1]}|\partial_{u}^{k}f(u)|\,,

(2.17)

where
k\in\mdmathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
.
This means that
\,\mdmathbb S^{\prime}
is the set of all linear functionals
f:\mdmathbb S\to\mdmathbb R
which are continuous with respect to all the seminorms
\|\cdot\|_{k}
.

We now fix a hydrodynamic profile
\rho(u,s)
solution of (
2.5
). In what follows, we will need to consider
T_{s}
as the semigroup associated to the hydrodynamic equation but satisfying a linearization of the boundary conditions of (
2.5
), that is, given an initial condition
H
,
T_{s}H
is the solution of

\begin{cases}&\partial_{s}T_{s}H(u)=\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}_{u}\,T_{s}H(u),\quad(s,u)\in[0,t]\times(0,1),\\
&\partial_{u}T_{s}H(-1)=jK(1-\rho(-1,s))^{K-1}T_{s}H(-1),\quad s\in[0,t]\\
&\partial_{u}T_{s}H(1)=-jK\rho(1,s)^{K-1}T_{s}H(1),\quad s\in[0,t]\\
&T_{0}H(\cdot)=H(\cdot),\end{cases}

(2.18)

Observe that above the boundary conditions can be rewritten as

\partial_{u}T_{s}H(-1)=\widetilde{D}^{\prime}_{-}(\rho(-1,s))T_{s}H(-1),\quad\partial_{u}T_{s}H(1)=\widetilde{D}^{\prime}_{+}(\rho(1,s))T_{s}H(1).

2.3.2
The density fluctuation field

We first introduce some notation. Recall that we denote by
\mdmathbb P_{\mu^{\epsilon}}
the probability measure in the Skorohod space denoted by
\mathcal{D}([0,T],\Lambda_{N})
induced by the Markov process
\{\eta_{t}\}_{t\geq{0}}
and the initial probability measure
\mu^{\epsilon}
and
\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}
denotes the expectation with respect to
\mdmathbb P_{\mu^{\epsilon}}
and that
\rho^{\epsilon}_{t}(x)=\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[\eta_{t}(x)]
.

Definition 2.10

(Density fluctuation field)
.

We define the density fluctuation field
Y_{\cdot}^{\epsilon}
as the time-trajectory of linear functionals acting on functions
H\in\mdmathbb S
as

Y^{\epsilon}_{t}(H)\;=\;\sqrt{\epsilon}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}H_{t}(\epsilon x)\Big(\eta_{t}(x)-\rho^{\epsilon}_{t}(x)\Big).

(2.19)

For each
\epsilon>0
, let
\mdmathbb{Q}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}
be the probability measure on
\mathcal{D}([0,T],\mdmathbb{S}^{\prime})
induced by the
density fluctuation field
Y_{\cdot}^{\epsilon}

and the measure
\mu^{\epsilon}
.
For every
n
positive integer,

C_{t}^{n,\epsilon}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}):=\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\Big[\Big(\eta_{t}(x_{1})-\rho^{\epsilon}_{t}(x_{1})\Big)\dots\Big(\eta_{t}(x_{n})-\rho^{\epsilon}_{t}(x_{n})\Big)\Big]

(2.20)

Moreover, we assume that

Assumption 1.
The initial measure
\mu^{\epsilon}
is a product measure that satisfies

\rho^{\epsilon}_{0}(x)=\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[\eta(y)]=u_{0}(\epsilon y),\textrm{\;for all\;}y\in\Lambda_{N}.

(2.21)

Above
u_{0}:[-1,1]\to[0,1]
is the initial condition of (
2.10
) and we assume that it is a smooth profile.
In contrast to the weaker assumption on the initial state given in (
2.13
), last assumption is essential for proving our results, i.e., Theorem
2.19
and
2.24
.

Remark 2.11
.

From
Assumption 1
, we conclude that
\rho_{0}^{\epsilon}(y)=\rho_{\epsilon}(0,y)
for all
y\in\Lambda_{N}.

One of the consequences of this assumption is that we are able to control the discrete gradient of the solution of (
2.10
) as stated in the next lemma, whose proof is postponed to Appendix
D
. This is a more refined estimate with respect to (3.8)-(3.9) in
[
6
]
.
To establish the estimates for various space–time dependent quantities in this work, we must address two main difficulties with respect to previous works. The first one is to show that those quantities are of order
\epsilon
or
\epsilon^{1+\zeta}
,
\zeta>0
. This is needed in order to have a closure of the equation and identify the limit. The second is to retain a time exponent so that the resulting expressions remain integrable after integrating with respect to time. By imposing
Assumption 1
, the second difficulty can be resolved, providing a time exponent strictly greater than
-1
and thus integrable close to 0. The same assumption is imposed in
[
3
]
and in Theorem 2 of
[
4
]
and in Theorem 2.1 in
[
5
]
.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
x\in\{-N,\dots,N-1\}
and
0\leq t\leq T
. Under
Assumption 1
, for any
\epsilon<1
, there exists a constant
C
such that

\displaystyle|\rho_{\epsilon}(t,x)-\rho_{\epsilon}(t,x+1)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{c_{T}\epsilon}+\epsilon^{-1}\frac{j}{2}\int_{0}^{t}d\lambda\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon^{-2}\lambda}+1}\sum_{z\in I_{\pm}}G_{\epsilon^{-2}\lambda}(x,z).

(2.22)

Above
G_{\epsilon^{-2}\lambda}(x,z)
denotes the heat kernel given in (
B.2
).
Let
\mathcal{D}([0,T],\mdmathbb S^{\prime})
(resp.
\mathcal{C}([0,T],\mdmathbb S^{\prime})
) be the space of cadlag (continuous) trajectories taking values in
\mdmathbb S^{\prime}
. For each
\epsilon>0
, let
\mdmathbb Q_{\epsilon}
be the probability measure on
\mathcal{D}([0,T],\mdmathbb S^{\prime})
induced by the density fluctuation field
Y^{\epsilon}
and the measure
\mu^{\epsilon}
.

Definition 2.13
.

Let
\rho_{t}
be the solution of the hydrodynamic equation (
2.5
). We sat that a stochastic process
{Y}
in the space of continuous distributions
\mathcal{C}([0,T],\mdmathbb{S}^{\prime})
is the formal solution of the equation:

\partial_{t}{Y}_{t}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{u}^{2}{Y}_{t}dt+\sqrt{\chi(\rho_{t})}\nabla W_{t}\,,

(2.23)

where
W_{t}
is a space-time white noise of unit variance and
\chi(u)=u(1-u)
, if:

i)

for every
H\in\mdmathbb{S}
, the stochastic processes
\{{M}_{t}(H),t\in[0,T]\}
and
\{{N}_{t}(H),t\in[0,T]\}
given by

\begin{split}&{M}_{t}(H):={Y}_{t}(H)-{Y}_{0}(H)-\int_{0}^{t}{Y}_{s}\left(\Delta H\right)ds,\\
&{N}_{t}(H):={M}_{t}(H)^{2}-t\left\|\nabla H\right\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{t})}^{2}\end{split}

(2.24)

are
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-martingales, where
\mathcal{F}_{t}:=\sigma\{{Y}_{s}(H):s\leq t,H\in\mdmathbb{S}\}
,

ii)

{Y}_{0}
is a mean-zero Gaussian field with covariance given, on
H,G\in\mdmathbb{S}
, by

\mdmathbb E\left[{Y}_{0}(H){Y}_{0}(G)\right]=\sigma(H,G).

(2.25)

Above, for
H,G\in\mdmathbb S
we denote the norm
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{t})}
associated to the inner product defined by

\begin{split}\langle H_{t},G_{t}\rangle_{\mdmathbb L^{2}(\rho_{t})}=&\int_{0}^{1}\chi(\rho(u,t))\partial_{u}H_{t}(u)\partial_{u}G_{t}(u)du\\
+&\widetilde{D}_{+}(\rho(1,t))H_{t}(1)G_{t}(1)+\widetilde{D}_{-}(\rho(-1,t))H_{t}(-1)G_{t}(-1),\end{split}

(2.26)

where
\rho_{t}(u)
is the solution of the hydrodynamic equation.

Now we comment about the uniqueness in law of the solution of the martingale problem given above. From the proof of Theorem
2.14
, which involves a compactness argument, we will prove the existence of a process that solves that martingale problem. In order to conclude the full convergence result we need to establish the uniqueness in law of such process. We do not present the complete proof of the uniqueness but we outline the strategy to be pursued, which follows similar steps to those of the proof of Proposition 5.1 of
[
9
]
. The idea is to frame the PDE (
2.18
) as a regular problem in the classical Sturm-Liouville theory. This is possible given the fact that the solution of the hydrodynamic equation is a continuous function of time, as discussed in Section 2.4, formula (12), in
[
4
]
. We can then obtain a similar result to that of Lemma 5.2 of
[
9
]
which is a consequence of Proposition 3.1 and Corollaries 3.2 and 3.3 of
[
9
]
. We leave the details to the reader.

Theorem 2.14

(Non-equilibrium fluctuations: the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck limit)
.

The sequence
\{\mdmathbb Q_{\epsilon}\}_{\epsilon}
converges, as
\epsilon\to 0
, to a generalized Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process solution of (
2.23
). As a consequence, the covariance of the limit field
{Y}_{t}
is given on
H,G\in{\mdmathbb S}
by

E\,[{Y}_{t}(H){Y}_{s}(G)]\;=\;\sigma(T_{t}H,T_{s}G)+\int_{0}^{s}\langle T_{t-r}H,T_{s-r}G\rangle_{\mdmathbb L^{2}(\rho_{r})}dr,

(2.27)

where

\sigma(T_{t}F,T_{s}G):=\int_{0}^{1}\chi(u_{0}(u))T_{t}f(u)T_{s}g(u)du,

T_{t}
is the solution of (
2.18
),
\chi(u)=u(1-u)
and
for
r>0
and
H,G\in\mdmathbb S
the inner product
\langle H_{s},G_{s}\rangle_{\mdmathbb L^{2}(\rho_{r})}
was defined above and
\rho(u,r)
is the solution of the hydrodynamic equation.

2.4
Estimates on
v
-functions

The proof of Theorem
2.14
relies on the analysis of the auxiliary martingales defined in (
3.3
)–(
3.4
). In this context, the space
v
-function defined below naturally arises as it controls the difference between the expectation of
\eta_{t}(x)
and

\rho_{\epsilon}(x,t)
. Obtaining appropriate estimates for this function is therefore essential. As we explain later in this section, determining suitable bounds for the
v
-function constitutes a crucial step in the overall analysis.

Definition 2.15
.

We fix an initial product measure
\mu^{\epsilon}
on
\Lambda_{N}
.
Suppose that the process
\{\eta_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
starts with
\mu_{\epsilon}
and
\rho_{\epsilon}(t,x)
is the solution of (
2.10
). We define the space
v
-function as:

v_{n}^{\epsilon}(\underline{x},t|\mu^{\epsilon}):=\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\eta_{t}(x_{i})-\rho_{\epsilon}(x_{i},t)\right)\right],\quad\underline{x}\in\Lambda_{N}^{n,\neq},n\geq 1

(2.28)

where
\Lambda_{N}^{n,\neq}
is the set of all sequences
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\Lambda_{N}^{n}
with
x_{i}\neq x_{j}
. For brevity, we
shall write
v_{n}^{\epsilon}(\underline{x},t)\equiv v_{n}^{\epsilon}(\underline{x},t|\mu^{\epsilon})
. When
\underline{x}=x
then

v_{1}^{\epsilon}(x,t):=\rho_{t}^{\epsilon}(x)-\rho_{\epsilon}(x,t).

(2.29)

and when
\underline{x}=\underline{\emptyset}
is the empty configuration (i.e., no particles in the system), then

v_{0}^{\epsilon}(\underline{\emptyset},t)=1.

(2.30)

We observe that as a consequence of Remark
2.11
we have
v^{\epsilon}_{1}(x,0)\equiv 0
. This will play an important role in what follows.

The notation
v_{n}^{\epsilon}(\underline{x},t)
with
\underline{x}\in\Lambda_{N}^{n,\neq}
is adopted for convenience when working with the duality and the labeled process (Definition
C.2
in Appendix
C
). However,
v_{n}^{\epsilon}(\underline{x},t)
can equivalently be written as

v_{n}^{\epsilon}(X,t)
, with
X
a non-empty subset of
\Lambda_{N}
since it is symmetric with respect to permutations of the coordinates in
\underline{x}
. Throughout the paper, we will interchange between the two definitions whenever it simplifies the presentation.

A key result on the space correlation
v
-function, is the following estimate for
v_{n}^{\epsilon}(x,t)
proved in
[
4
]
which depends on the number of particles
n
.

Theorem 2.16
.

[
6
, Theorem 2.1]

Let
K
be a fixed integer number such that
K\geq 2
as given in Section
2.1
. Then, there exist
\tau>0
such that for any
0<c^{*}<\frac{1}{4(K+2)}
the following holds: For any
\beta^{*}>0
and for any positive integer
n
, there is a constant
c_{n}<\infty
, so that for every
\epsilon>0
, any initial product measure
\mu^{\epsilon}
,

\sup_{\underline{x}\in\Lambda_{N}^{n,\neq}}|v_{n}^{\epsilon}(\underline{x},t|\mu^{\epsilon})|\leq\begin{cases}c_{n}(\epsilon^{-2}t)^{-c^{*}n},&\text{for }t\leq\epsilon^{\beta^{*}}\\
c_{n}\epsilon^{(2-\beta^{*})c^{*}n},&\text{for }\epsilon^{\beta^{*}}\leq t\leq\tau\log\epsilon^{-1}.\end{cases}

(2.31)

The next corollary is direct consequence of Theorem
2.16
and the proof is given in Appendix
C.2
.

Corollary 2.17
.

Let
\beta^{*}<2
and
c^{*}<\frac{1}{4(K+2)}
. There exists a positive constant
c
such that the following estimate holds

\sup_{0<t\leq T}\sup_{\underline{x}\in\Lambda_{N}^{n,\neq}}|v^{\epsilon}_{n}(\underline{x},t|\mu^{\epsilon})|\leq c\epsilon^{(2-\beta^{*})c^{*}n},\quad n\geq 1.

(2.32)

The estimate given in Theorem
2.16
proves insufficient for our purposes-specifically, for achieving closure of the auxiliary martingales presented in Section
3.1
. Therefore, refined estimates are provided in Theorems
2.19
and
2.24
, which form the core contributions of this work. Before we proceed, we note that when
K=1
the result below is not necessary for the proof of non-equilibrium fluctuations as it was given in
[
9
,
10
]
. Nevertheless, when
2\leq K\leq N
we need a much more refined analysis not only for the closure of the martingale but also to show tightness of the sequence of the fluctuation fields.
The next corollary is a direct consequence of Corollary
2.17
and the proof is omitted.

Corollary 2.18
.

Let
K\geq 2
be a fixed integer number,
\zeta>0
and
0<c^{*}<\frac{1}{4(K+2)}
. For
\beta^{*}<2
and
\epsilon<1
, there exists
N^{*}
positive integer (large enough) satisfying

(2-\beta^{*})c^{*}(N^{*}+1)>1+\zeta

(2.33)

and thus

\sup_{0<t\leq T}\sup_{\underline{x}\in\Lambda_{N}^{n,\neq}}|v^{\epsilon}_{n}(\underline{x},t)|\leq c\epsilon^{1+\zeta},\quad n\geq N^{*}+1.

(2.34)

Before we proceed we note that the estimates for the space
v
-function given in the next theorem, continue to be insufficient when
n=1,2
as we do not get a bound of order
\epsilon
contrarily to the case
K=1
, where in Proposition 8.1 of
[
9
]
it was derived a bound of order
\epsilon
for the two point correlation function. That bound was sufficient to derive the non-equilibrium fluctuations of the model in the case
K=1
. Consequently, the next result and all results that follow, are stated for
K\geq 2
. The first result on space
v
-functions can be stated as follows.

Theorem 2.19

(Space correlation
v
-estimate)
.

Let
K\geq 2
be a fixed integer number, and
\zeta>0
. For any initial product measure
\mu^{\epsilon}
satisfying (
2.21
), and any time
0<t\leq T

\sup_{\underline{x}\in\Lambda_{N}^{n,\neq}}|v_{n}^{\epsilon}(\underline{x},t|\mu^{\epsilon})|\lesssim\mathbf{1}_{n=1,2}\epsilon^{1-2\zeta}+\mathbf{1}_{3\leq n\leq K}\epsilon+\mathbf{1}_{n\geq K+1}\epsilon^{1+\zeta}.

(2.35)

Remark 2.20
.

Above and in the following,
\lesssim
denotes an inequality that is correct up to a
multiplicative universal constant, that is, independent of
N,\epsilon,t
. This means that the result above is not time dependent. This will be crucial ahead.

Remark 2.21
.

A simple computation shows that the estimates obtained above for the space
v
-function are also true for the correlation function
C_{t}^{n,\epsilon}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
defined in (
2.20
).

From the last result we can conclude to the next bounds that we state as a corollary for future convenience. We do not present the proof as it follows easily from the proof of the previous result.

Corollary 2.22
.

Let
K\geq 2
be a fixed integer number and
\zeta>0
. For any initial product measure
\mu^{\epsilon}
satisfying (
2.21
), and time
0<t\leq T

\sup_{\underline{x}\in\Lambda_{N}^{n,\neq}}|v^{\epsilon}_{n}(\underline{x},t|\mu^{\epsilon})|)-v^{\epsilon}_{n}(\underline{x}+\underline{e}_{1},t|\mu^{\epsilon})|)\lesssim\begin{cases}\epsilon,&\;1\leq n\leq K\\
\epsilon^{1+\zeta},&n\geq K+1,\end{cases}

(2.36)

where
\underline{e}_{1}
being the unit vector in the positive 1st-direction.

In our case, since the boundary dynamics increases the degree of polynomial functions of
\eta
the analysis is much more complicated and we cannot get the bound
\epsilon
as desired. To overcome that issue, we instead gain a better rate of convergence to
0
by analyzing space-time
v
-functions that we define as follows.

Definition 2.23
.

Let
\underline{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\Lambda_{N}^{n,\neq}
and
\underline{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{m})\in\Lambda_{N}^{m,\neq}
with
n,m\geq 1
. For any initial product measure
\mu^{\epsilon}
and times
0<s<r
, the space-time correlation
v
-function is given by

v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,r}(\underline{y},\underline{x}|\mu^{\epsilon}):=\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{m}\bar{\eta}_{s}(y_{j})\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bar{\eta}_{r}(x_{i})\right]

(2.37)

where
\bar{\eta}_{t}(x):=\eta_{t}(x)-\rho_{\epsilon}(x,t)
,
t\geq 0
.

The subscript indicates the times
s,r
involved in the space-time correlation
v
-function. The inputs
(\underline{y},\underline{x})
are the positions of the
m
particles at time
s
, denoted by
\underline{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{m})
and the positions of the
n
particles at time
r
, denoted by
\underline{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
.
The superscript reflects the dependence on
\epsilon
, which may sometimes be omitted, and the number of particles at time
s
and at time
r
, i.e.,
m
and
n
respectively.
In Theorem
2.24
, we establish a space-time correlation estimate. This theorem, in conjunction with Proposition
D.1
, demonstrates that the
\mdmathbb{L}^{2}
-norm of (
3.12
)–(
3.15
) are bounded by quantities that vanish as
\epsilon\to 0
. In Section
3.1
, we revisit the estimation of these terms, providing detailed calculations to complete the analysis.

Theorem 2.24

(Space-time correlation
v
-estimate)
.

Let
K\geq 2
be a fixed integer number,
n,m\geq 1
and
\zeta>0
. For any initial product measure
\mu^{\epsilon}
satisfying (
2.21
), and times
0<s<r\leq T
, there exists
\tilde{\Delta}>0
(independent of
\epsilon
), such that when
r-s\leq\tilde{\Delta}

\sup_{\underline{y}\in\Lambda_{N}^{m,\neq}}\sup_{\underline{x}\in\Lambda_{N}^{n,\neq}}|v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,r}(\underline{y},\underline{x}|\mu^{\epsilon})|\lesssim\begin{cases}\epsilon(r-s)^{-1/2},&n+m=2,3\\
\epsilon^{1+\zeta}(r-s)^{-1+\zeta},&4\leq n+m\leq K\\
\epsilon^{1+\zeta},&n\geq K+1,m\geq 1.\end{cases}

(2.38)

When
r-s>\tilde{\Delta}
,

\sup_{\underline{y}\in\Lambda_{N}^{m,\neq}}\sup_{\underline{x}\in\Lambda_{N}^{n,\neq}}|v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,r}(\underline{y},\underline{x}|\mu^{\epsilon})|\lesssim\begin{cases}\epsilon,&n+m=2,3\\
\epsilon^{1+\zeta/2},&4\leq n+m\leq K\\
\epsilon^{1+\zeta},&n\geq K+1,m\geq 1.\end{cases}

(2.39)

The proof of the theorem is given in Section
5.2
.
As we did for the space
v
-function we summarize in the next corollary some important bounds.

Corollary 2.25
.

Let
K\geq 2
be a fixed integer number,
n,m\geq 1
and
\zeta>0
. For any initial product measure
\mu^{\epsilon}
satisfying (
2.21
), and times
0<s<r\leq T
, there exists
\tilde{\Delta}>0
, such that when
r-s\leq\tilde{\Delta}

\sup_{\underline{y}\in\Lambda_{N}^{m,\neq}}\sup_{\underline{x}\in\Lambda_{N}^{n,\neq}}|v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,r}(\underline{y},\underline{x}|\mu^{\epsilon})-v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,r}(\underline{y},\underline{x}+\underline{e}_{1}|\mu^{\epsilon})|\lesssim\begin{cases}\epsilon^{1+\zeta}(r-s)^{-1+\zeta},&n+m=2\\
\epsilon^{1+\zeta}(r-s)^{-1/2},&n=1,m\geq 2\\
\epsilon^{1+\zeta}(r-s)^{-1+\zeta},&2\leq n\leq K,m\geq 1\\
\epsilon^{1+\zeta},&n\geq K+1,m\geq 1\end{cases}

(2.40)

and when
r-s>\tilde{\Delta}
,

\sup_{\underline{y}\in\Lambda_{N}^{m,\neq}}\sup_{\underline{x}\in\Lambda_{N}^{n,\neq}}|v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,r}(\underline{y},\underline{x}|\mu^{\epsilon})-v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,r}(\underline{y},\underline{x}+\underline{e}_{1}|\mu^{\epsilon})|)\lesssim\epsilon^{1+\zeta}.

(2.41)

where
\underline{e}_{1}
being the unit vector in the positive 1st-direction.

The statement of last corollary is similar to Corollary
4.13
for the space
v
-function, and it follows directly from the proof of Theorem
2.24
.

3
Proof of Theorem
2.14

3.1
Auxiliary martingales

Fix
t\in[0,T]
and let
\psi
be a function in
C^{1,2}([0,T]\times[-1,1])
. For now we do not assume anything else on this function, but below we will see that we will need to impose other conditions on
\psi
in order to obtain that the limit field satisfies

{Y}_{t}(H)\;=\;{Y}_{0}(T_{t}H)+W_{t}(H),

(3.1)

for any
H\in\mdmathbb S
, where

T_{t}
is the solution of (
2.18
) and
W_{t}(H)
is a mean zero Gaussian variable with variance

\int_{0}^{t}\|T_{t-s}H\|^{2}_{\mdmathbb L^{2}(\rho_{s})}ds.

(3.2)

From
[
14
, p. 330]
we know that

\displaystyle M_{t}^{\epsilon}({}_{t})

\displaystyle\;:=\;\ Y_{t}^{\epsilon}({}_{t})-Y^{\epsilon}_{0}({}_{0})-\int_{0}^{t}\Lambda_{s}^{\epsilon}({}_{s})\,ds\,,

(3.3)

\displaystyle N_{t}^{\epsilon}({}_{t})

\displaystyle\;:=\;(M_{t}^{\epsilon}({}_{t}))^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma^{\epsilon}_{s}({}_{s})\,ds

(3.4)

are martingales with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{t}
, where

\begin{split}&\Lambda_{s}^{\epsilon}({}_{s})\;:=\;(\partial_{s}+L_{\epsilon})Y_{s}^{\epsilon}({}_{s})\,,\\
&\Gamma^{\epsilon}({}_{s})\;:=\;L_{\epsilon}Y_{s}^{\epsilon}({}_{s})^{2}-2Y_{s}^{N}({}_{s})L_{\epsilon}Y_{s}^{\epsilon}({}_{s})\,.\end{split}

From the computations in Appendix
A.1
we see that

\begin{split}\Lambda_{s}^{\epsilon}({}_{s})\;&=\;Y_{s}^{\epsilon}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{\epsilon}{}_{s}+\partial_{s}{}_{s}\Big)\\
&+\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}\sum_{x\in I_{+}}{}_{s}(\epsilon x)\Big(D_{+}\eta_{s}(x)-\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[D_{+}\eta_{s}(x)]\Big)\\
&-\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}\sum_{x\in I_{-}}{}_{s}(\epsilon x)\Big(D_{-}\eta_{s}(x)-\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[D_{-}\eta_{s}(x)]\Big).\end{split}

(3.5)

Now we isolate the boundary contribution from the first term on the right-hand side of the last display and obtain:

\begin{split}\Lambda_{s}^{\epsilon}({}_{s})\;&=\;\sqrt{\epsilon}\sum_{x=-(N-1)}^{N-1}\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{\epsilon}{}_{s}(\epsilon x)\Big(\eta_{s}(x)-\rho^{\epsilon}_{s}(x)\Big)+Y_{s}^{\epsilon}(\partial_{s}{}_{s})\\
&+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}\nabla_{\epsilon}^{+}{}_{s}(-1)\bar{\eta}_{s}(-N)-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}\nabla_{\epsilon}^{-}{}_{s}(1)\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)\\
&+\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}\sum_{x\in I_{+}}{}_{s}(\epsilon x)\Big(D_{+}\eta_{s}(x)-\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[D_{+}\eta_{s}(x)]\Big)\\
&-\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}\sum_{x\in I_{-}}{}_{s}(\epsilon x)\Big(D_{-}\eta_{s}(x)-\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[D_{-}\eta_{s}(x)]\Big).\end{split}

(3.6)

Note that the first term on the right-hand side of the last display will give rise macroscopically, when sending
\epsilon\to 0
, to the term
Y_{s}(\Delta{}_{s})
, since the discrete Laplacian will converge to the continuous one.

Now we analyze the third line of the last display. The analysis of the fourth line is completely analogous.
Observe that by a Taylor expansion of
s
around
1
, line of last display is equal to

\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)\sum_{x\in I_{+}}\Big(D_{+}\eta_{s}(x)-\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[D_{+}\eta_{s}(x)]\Big)+O_{\psi,K}(\sqrt{\epsilon}).

(3.7)

At this point, we specify
K=2
to make the presentation simpler, but the reader is invited to follow the computations presented in the Appendix
A.2
, and obtain our results for any value of
K
.
In this setting, note that
I_{+}=\{N-1,N\}
and
D_{+}\eta(N)=(1-\eta(N))
and
D_{+}\eta(N-1)=(1-\eta(N-1))\eta(N)
. Therefore the term on the left-hand side of (
3.7
) writes as

\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)\Big(\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[\eta_{s}(N)\eta_{s}(N-1)]-\eta_{s}(N)\eta_{s}(N-1)\Big).

(3.8)

At this point we observe that in order to continue we will rewrite the martingales in terms of the variables centered with respect to
\rho_{\epsilon}
as they appear in the
v
-functions estimates.
To that end, note that

\eta_{s}(N)\eta_{s}(N-1)=\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)\bar{\eta}_{s}(N-1)+\rho_{\epsilon}(s,N)\eta_{s}(N-1)+\rho_{\epsilon}(N-1,s)\eta_{s}(N)-\rho_{\epsilon}(N,s)\rho_{\epsilon}(N-1,s),

where
\bar{\eta}_{s}(x)=\eta_{s}(x)-\rho_{\epsilon}(x,s)
.
Recall that

\begin{split}v_{2}^{\epsilon}(N-1,N,s|\mu^{\epsilon})=\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[(\eta_{s}(N-1)-\rho_{\epsilon}(N-1,s))(\eta_{s}(N)-\rho_{\epsilon}(N,s))].\end{split}

Therefore,

\begin{split}\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[\eta_{s}(N)\eta_{s}(N-1)]-\eta_{s}(N)\eta_{s}(N-1)&=v_{2}^{\epsilon}(N-1,N,s|\mu^{\epsilon})-\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)\bar{\eta}_{s}(N-1)\\
&+\rho_{\epsilon}(N,s)\Big(\rho^{\epsilon}_{s}(N-1)-\eta_{s}(N-1)\Big)\\
&+\rho_{\epsilon}(N-1,s)(\rho^{\epsilon}_{s}(N)-\eta_{s}(N)\Big).\end{split}

Simple computations show that the last display is equal to

\begin{split}&v_{2}^{\epsilon}(N-1,N,s|\mu^{\epsilon})-\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)\bar{\eta}_{s}(N-1)+\rho_{\epsilon}(N,s)v_{1}^{\epsilon}(N-1,s|\mu^{\epsilon})+\rho_{\epsilon}(N-1,s)v_{1}^{\epsilon}(N,s|\mu^{\epsilon})\\
&-\rho_{\epsilon}(N,s)[\bar{\eta}_{s}(N-1)-\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)]-(\rho_{\epsilon}(N-1,s)-\rho_{\epsilon}(N,s))\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)\\
&+2\Big(\rho_{s}(1)-\rho_{\epsilon}(N,s)\Big)\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)-2\rho_{s}(1)\bar{\eta}_{s}(N).\end{split}

From this we can write the time integral in (
3.3
) as

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}Y_{s}^{\epsilon}(\Delta{}_{s}+\partial_{s}{}_{s})-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}\nabla_{\epsilon}^{-}{}_{s}(1)\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)-2\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)\rho_{s}(1)\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)ds

(3.9)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)v_{2}^{\epsilon}(N-1,N,s|\mu^{\epsilon})ds

(3.10)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)\Big\{\rho_{\epsilon}(N,s)v_{1}^{\epsilon}(N-1,s|\mu^{\epsilon})+\rho_{\epsilon}(N-1,s)v_{1}^{\epsilon}(N,s|\mu^{\epsilon})\Big\}ds

(3.11)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)\rho_{\epsilon}(N,s)[\bar{\eta}_{s}(N-1)-\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)]ds

(3.12)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)(\rho_{\epsilon}(N-1,s)-\rho_{\epsilon}(N,s))\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)ds

(3.13)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)\Big\{2\Big(\rho_{s}(1)-\rho_{\epsilon}(N,s)\Big)\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)\Big\}ds

(3.14)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)\bar{\eta}_{s}(N-1)ds

(3.15)

plus similar terms regarding the left boundary plus terms that vanish as
\epsilon\to 0
. Our goal is now to show that, as
\epsilon\to 0
, all terms–except for the leftmost one in the first line of the last display–vanish in the
\mdmathbb L^{2}
-norm. We note that our approach consists in taking the terms above that involve
v
-functions and estimating them directly with the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. For the remaining terms we use
\mdmathbb L^{2}
bounds and estimates on space-time
v
-functions. Before that, note that from a Taylor expansion of the function
\psi
at
x=1
, we can write the two rightmost terms in (
3.9
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{t}-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}\nabla_{\epsilon}^{-}{}_{s}(1)\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)-2\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)\rho_{s}(1)\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)ds\\
=&\int_{0}^{t}{-}\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}\Big\{{}^{\prime}_{s}(1)+\epsilon{}^{\prime\prime}(\xi)\Big\}\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)-2\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)\rho_{s}(1)\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)ds\end{split}

(3.16)

where
\xi\in(1-\epsilon,1)
. Since
\psi\in\mdmathbb S
, i.e., in particular,

\partial_{u}{}_{s}(1)=-2j\rho_{s}(1){}_{s}(1)
for any
s\in[0,t]
, the last display is equal to

\displaystyle\begin{split}{-}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{2}{}^{\prime\prime}(\xi)\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)ds.\end{split}

(3.17)

Since the occupation variables are bounded,
the
\mdmathbb L^{2}
-norm of last display can be bounded by a term of order
O(\epsilon)
and it vanishes as
\epsilon\to 0
.

3.1.1
Estimation of (
3.10
) and (
3.11
)

We estimate the
\mdmathbb{L}^{2}
-norm of the terms (
3.10
) and (
3.11
) using Theorem
2.19
along with the fact that
|\rho_{\epsilon}(\cdot,s)|\leq 1
.
Note that, from Theorem
2.19
, precisely from (
2.35
) with
n=2,
we bound the term (
3.10
) from above by

\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{t}\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)v_{2}^{\epsilon}(N-1,N,s|\mu^{\epsilon})ds\lesssim\frac{\epsilon^{1-2\zeta}t}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}.\end{split}

Similarly, we prove that (
3.11
) is bounded as

\int_{0}^{t}\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)\Big\{\rho_{\epsilon}(N,s)v_{1}^{\epsilon}(N-1,s|\mu^{\epsilon})+\rho_{\epsilon}(N-1,s)v_{1}^{\epsilon}(N,s|\mu^{\epsilon})\Big\}ds\lesssim\epsilon^{1/2-2\zeta}t.

For
\zeta<1/4
, the
\mdmathbb L^{2}
- norm of the above terms vanish as
\epsilon\to 0
.

3.1.2
Estimation of (
3.12
)-(
3.15
)

Unlike the terms in (
3.10
) and (
3.11
), which are deterministic, the remaining terms do not involve space correlation functions
v_{n}^{\epsilon}
but instead, they depend on products of
\bar{\eta}
’s.
Since these terms are more intricate than the previous ones, we now provide a comprehensive analysis of estimating (
3.12
)–(
3.15
). Lemmas
3.1
–
3.4
provide the precise estimates which are proved by primarily using Theorem
2.24
and results from
[
4
]
highlighted in the subsequent analysis. Each estimate is presented in detail below, demonstrating that the terms (
3.12
)–(
3.15
) vanish in the
\mdmathbb{L}^{2}
-norm as
\epsilon\to 0
. This is provided under a sufficiently small parameter
\zeta>0
appearing in Lemmas
3.1
–
3.4
.

The estimate of the
\mdmathbb{L}^{2}
-norm of (
3.12
) is given in the next lemma.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
t\in(0,T]
. For any
x,y\in I_{+}
(similarly for
x,y\in I_{-}
) with
|x-y|=1
and for any
\zeta>0
, it holds

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91684pt\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{t}\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)\rho_{\epsilon}(x,s)[\bar{\eta}_{s}(y)-\bar{\eta}_{s}(x)]ds\right)^{2}\right]\lesssim\epsilon^{\zeta}\left(t^{1+\zeta}\mathbf{1}_{t<1}+t^{2}\mathbf{1}_{t\geq 1}\right).

Remark 3.2
.

We note that the previous lemma also holds when the points
x,y\notin I_{\pm}
, because it is a consequence of the bounds of Corollary
2.25
which hold more generally.

Proof.

Observe that by squaring the time integral and using Fubini’s theorem, we can bound the expectation appearing in the statement as

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{t}\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)\rho_{\epsilon}(x,s)[\bar{\eta}_{s}(y)-\bar{\eta}_{s}(x)]ds\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=2\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{t}dr\int_{0}^{r}ds\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\left[\left(\bar{\eta}_{s}(x)-\bar{\eta}_{s}(y)\right)\left(\bar{\eta}_{r}(x)-\bar{\eta}_{r}(y)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=2\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{t}dr\int_{0}^{r}ds\left(v^{\epsilon,1,1}_{s,r}(x,x)-v^{\epsilon,1}_{s,r}(x,y)-v^{\epsilon,1,1}_{s,r}(y,x)+v^{\epsilon,1,1}_{s,r}(y,y)\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{t}dr\int_{0}^{r}ds\sup_{x,y\in\Lambda_{N}}|v^{\epsilon,1,1}_{s,r}(y,x|\mu^{\epsilon})-v^{\epsilon,1,1}_{s,r}(y,x+1|\mu^{\epsilon})|)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{t}dr\int_{0}^{r}ds\sup_{x,y\in\Lambda_{N}}|v^{\epsilon,1,1}_{s,r}(y,x|\mu^{\epsilon})-v^{\epsilon,1,1}_{s,r}(y,x+1|\mu^{\epsilon})|){\bf 1}_{r-s\leq\tilde{\Delta}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{t}dr\int_{0}^{r}ds\sup_{x,y\in\Lambda_{N}}|v^{\epsilon,1,1}_{s,r}(y,x|\mu^{\epsilon})-v^{\epsilon,1,1}_{s,r}(y,x+1|\mu^{\epsilon})|){\bf 1}_{r-s>\tilde{\Delta}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\epsilon^{\zeta}t^{1+\zeta}+\epsilon^{\zeta}t^{2}\lesssim\epsilon^{\zeta}\left(t^{1+\zeta}\mathbf{1}_{t<1}+t^{2}\mathbf{1}_{t\geq 1}\right),

(3.18)

where
\tilde{\Delta}
is given in Theorem
2.24
, and the last line follows from Corollary
2.25
.
∎

Now we head up to estimating the
\mdmathbb{L}^{2}
-norm of (
3.13
) with the use of the next lemma.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
t\in(0,T]
. For any
\zeta>0
, it holds

\displaystyle\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{t}\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)(\rho_{\epsilon}(N-1,s)-\rho_{\epsilon}(N,s))\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)ds\right)^{2}\right]\lesssim\epsilon^{1-4\zeta}t^{2}.

(3.19)

Proof.

The lemma follows directly from Corollary
D.2
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{t}\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)(\rho_{\epsilon}(s,N-1)-\rho_{\epsilon}(s,N))\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)ds\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\lesssim\frac{1}{\epsilon}\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{t}(\rho_{\epsilon}(N-1,s)-\rho_{\epsilon}(N,s))\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)ds\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=2\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{t}dr\int_{0}^{r}ds(\rho_{\epsilon}(N-1,s)-\rho_{\epsilon}(N,s))(\rho_{\epsilon}(N-1,r)-\rho_{\epsilon}(N,s))v^{\epsilon,1,1}_{s,r}(N,N)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq c\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{t}dr\int_{0}^{r}dsC_{T}^{2}\epsilon^{2-4\zeta}\lesssim\epsilon^{1-4\zeta}t^{2}

(3.20)

where we use that
|v^{\epsilon,1,1}_{s,r}(N,N)|\leq 1
.
∎

Finally, we estimate the
\mdmathbb{L}^{2}
-norm of (
3.15
) which is a consequence of the next lemma.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
t\in(0,T]
. Then, for any
\zeta>0
there is a constant
c>0
such that

\displaystyle\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{t}\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)\bar{\eta}_{s}(N-1)\right)^{2}\right]\lesssim\epsilon^{\zeta}\left(t^{1+\zeta}\mathbf{1}_{t<1}+t^{2}\mathbf{1}_{t\geq 1}\right).

(3.21)

Proof.

The estimate is a direct result of Theorem
2.24
for
n=m=2
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{t}\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)\bar{\eta}_{s}(N-1)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\lesssim\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{t}dr\int_{0}^{r}ds|v_{s,r}^{\epsilon,2,2}(N-1,N,N-1,N)|

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{t}dr\int_{0}^{r}ds\sup_{\underline{x}\in\Lambda_{N}^{2\neq}}|v_{s,r}^{\epsilon,2,2}(\underline{x},N-1,N)|

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{t}dr\int_{0}^{r}ds\sup_{\underline{x}\in\Lambda_{N}^{2\neq}}|v_{s,r}^{\epsilon,2,2}(\underline{x},N-1,N)|{\bf 1}_{r-s\leq\tilde{\Delta}}

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{t}dr\int_{0}^{r}ds\sup_{\underline{x}\in\Lambda_{N}^{2\neq}}|v_{s,r}^{\epsilon,2,2}(\underline{x},N-1,N)|{\bf 1}_{r-s>\tilde{\Delta}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\lesssim\epsilon^{1+\zeta}t^{1+\zeta}+\epsilon^{1+\zeta}t^{2}\lesssim\epsilon^{\zeta}\left(t^{1+\zeta}\mathbf{1}_{t<1}+t^{2}\mathbf{1}_{t\geq 1}\right).

∎

With this section, we complete the closure of the martingale decomposition for
K=2
given in (
3.5
) i.e.

\displaystyle M_{t}^{\epsilon}({}_{t})

\displaystyle\;:=\;\ Y_{t}^{\epsilon}({}_{t})-Y^{\epsilon}_{0}({}_{0})-\int_{0}^{t}Y_{s}^{\epsilon}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\Delta{}_{s}+\partial_{s}{}_{s}\Big)ds.

(3.22)

We observe that the last display can also be generalized to any
K
using the results of Appendix
A.1
, together with the results of Theorem
2.19
and
2.24
.
Now we discuss the conditions that we have to impose on the function
s
in order to recover (
3.1
).
Observe that taking
{}_{s}=T_{t-s}H
where
T_{t}
is solution of (
2.18
) and
H
is a
C^{2}
function, then from the first equality in (
2.18
), the rightmost term on the first line of last display vanishes, since
\partial_{s}{}_{s}=-\partial_{s}T_{t-s}H=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{u}^{2}T_{t-s}H
. Above we have already used the boundary conditions in (
2.18
) to control the contribution from the boundary generator. Up to here we have that

\displaystyle M_{t}^{\epsilon}(H)=\ Y_{t}^{\epsilon}(H)-Y^{\epsilon}_{0}(T_{t}H)

(3.23)

plus terms that vanish as
\epsilon\to 0
. Finally, note that
from Corollary

6.4
, the sequence of martingales
\{M_{t}^{\epsilon}(H);t\in[0,T]\}_{\epsilon}
converges in the topology of
\mathcal{D}([0,T],\mdmathbb R)
, as
\epsilon\to 0
, towards a mean-zero Gaussian process
W_{t}(H)
with quadratic variation given by

\int_{0}^{t}\|\ T_{t-s}H\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{s})}^{2}\,ds,

where
\rho(u,s)
is the solution of the hydrodynamic equation (
2.5
) and the
\mdmathbb L^{2}
-norm is defined in (
2.26
). Moreover, if we assume also that the sequence
\{Y_{t}^{\epsilon}\}_{\epsilon}
is tight, which will be proved later in Section
6
, then taking the limit through a subsequence
\{Y_{t}^{\epsilon}\}_{\epsilon_{k}}
, we see that the limit points satisfy (
3.1
)
as desired.

4
Estimates on space
v
-functions

This section focuses on the derivation of integral inequalities for the space
v
-functions that form a critical component in establishing our main results, namely Theorem
2.19
and
2.24
. While the integral inequalities for space
v
-functions were previously established in Section 5 of
[
6
]
, we revisit them here, as well as in Appendix
C
, since we need to improve the bounds for the case of small
n
(as was already discussed in the introduction) and to lay the groundwork for deriving the corresponding inequalities for space-time
v
-functions to be presented in Section
5
. Special emphasis is placed on the key aspects necessary for this extension. We observe that the core idea of the proof of Theorem
2.19
was originally developed in
[
3
]
, and it suffices to prove the boundedness of

\Big(\mathbf{1}_{n=1,2}\epsilon^{-1+2\zeta}+\mathbf{1}_{3\leq n\leq K}\epsilon^{-1}+\mathbf{1}_{n\geq K+1}\epsilon^{-1-\zeta}\Big)\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\sup_{\underline{x}\in\Lambda_{N}^{n,\neq}}|v_{n}^{\epsilon}(\underline{x},t)|.

To this end, our starting point is the integral solution of the evolution equation for the space
v
-functions, given in (
4.2
).
This integral solution essentially describes all possible ways in which particles can move within the system, that is, all possible scenarios that may occur, which are summarized as follows:

•

Two particles meet (i.e., occupy neighboring lattice sites), and one of them dies;

•

Two particles meet, and both die;

•

One or more particles reach the boundaries
I_{\pm}
, and either no new particles are created or new ones are born.

At the level of estimation, such events are mainly controlled using tools developed in
[
4
]
and
[
6
]
.
These methods rely on Gaussian kernel approximations for the aforementioned events, in particular by bounding the transition probability of the stirring process from a labeled configuration
\underline{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
to
\underline{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
by the product of the corresponding probabilities for
n
independent random walks, each estimated via Gaussian kernels.
Since the Gaussian kernels are further bounded by
\frac{1}{[\epsilon^{-2}t]^{1/2}+1}
after diffusive scaling, that is,

\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(\underline{x}\xrightarrow{\lambda}\underline{y})\leq\prod_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}P^{(\epsilon)}_{\lambda}(x_{i},y_{j})\lesssim\frac{1}{[\epsilon^{-2}\lambda]^{n/2}+1}\qquad\text{(Liggett's inequality)},

(4.1)

the product of more than one such kernels is not necessarily integrable over
[0,T]
.
This presents a major challenge in proving that the space
v
-function is at least of order
\epsilon
for
n\geq 3
, and, as we shall see later, at least of order
\epsilon
for the space–time
v
-function as well.
To overcome this difficulty, we estimate the possible scenarios in a different manner.
Depending on the number of particles that die and/or are born, the resulting integral inequality involves a
v
-function whose degree corresponds to the number of remaining particles. Therefore, the evolution equation for the space
v_{n}^{\epsilon}
- function includes terms involving
v_{j}^{\epsilon}
, where, according to the scenarios described above,
j=n-2,n-1
, or
j=n,n+1,\dots,n+K-1
. For this reason, to fully estimate all terms appearing in the integral solution of (
4.2
), we need to consider the integral solutions of all
v_{j}^{\epsilon}
. In this way, all
v_{j}^{\epsilon},\;j\geq 1
are estimated simultaneously, showing that as the degree of
v_{j}^{\epsilon}
increases, the estimates are bounded by the order
\epsilon^{1+\zeta}
.
The structure of the proof goes as follows: we start with the analysis of the evolution equation of

v
-functions, then proceed with the estimation of the scenarios mentioned above and conclude with simultaneous estimation of the vector of all
v_{j}^{\epsilon}
’s.

4.0.1
An evolution equation for the space
v
-function

We first recall, for example, from Lemma 5.1 of
[
6
]
, the evolution equation for space
v
-functions, for
X
such that
|X|=n
, given by

\frac{d}{dt}v_{n}^{\epsilon}(X,t)=\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\left[\;L_{\epsilon}\prod_{x\in X}\left\{\eta_{t}(x)-\rho_{\epsilon}(t,x)\right\}\right]+\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\left[\;\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\prod_{x\in X}\left\{\eta_{t}(x)-\rho_{\epsilon}(t,x)\right\}\right].

(4.2)

We calculate the right hand side of (
4.2
), obtaining the discrete equation for
v_{n}^{\epsilon}(X,t)
given by

\frac{d}{dt}v_{n}^{\epsilon}(X,t)=\epsilon^{-2}(L_{0}v_{n}^{\epsilon})(X,t)+(C_{\epsilon}v_{n}^{\epsilon})(X,t)

(4.3)

where

L_{0}v_{n}^{\epsilon}
denotes the action of
L_{0}
on
v_{n}^{\epsilon}(\cdot,t)
as a function of
X
with
X
regarded as a particle configuration. Also,
\epsilon^{-2}L_{0}
represents the generator of the symmetric stirring process that we represent by
(X(\lambda))_{\lambda\geq 0}
that is reflected at the boundary points
x\in\{-N,N\}
.
The operator
(C_{\epsilon}v_{n}^{\epsilon})
is the result of a linear operator acting on
v_{n}^{\epsilon}
, given as

(C_{\epsilon}v_{n}^{\epsilon})(X,t):=\epsilon^{-2}(Av_{n}^{\epsilon})(X,t)+\epsilon^{-1}(Bv_{n}^{\epsilon})(X,t),

(4.4)

with
(Av_{n}^{\epsilon})(X,t)=0
if
|X|<2
, while for
|X|\geq 2

\displaystyle(Av_{n}^{\epsilon})(X,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(A^{1}v_{n}^{\epsilon})(X,t)+(A^{2}v_{n}^{\epsilon})(X,t)

(4.5)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{x,y\in X}\mathbf{1}_{|x-y|=1}\left[\rho_{\epsilon}(t,x)-\rho_{\epsilon}(t,y)\right]\left[v_{n-1}^{\epsilon}(X\setminus x,t)-v_{n-1}^{\epsilon}(X\setminus y,t)\right]

\displaystyle+\sum_{x,y\in X}\mathbf{1}_{|x-y|=1}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\left[\rho_{\epsilon}(t,x)-\rho_{\epsilon}(t,y)\right]^{2}v_{n-2}^{\epsilon}(X\setminus(x\cup y),t)\right)

and
Bv_{n}^{\epsilon}=B_{+}v_{n}^{\epsilon}+B_{-}v_{n}^{\epsilon}
, with
(B_{\pm}v_{n}^{\epsilon})(X,t)
that can be written as

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69046pt(B_{\pm}v_{n}^{\epsilon})(X,t)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\sum_{p=1}^{\min\{{|X|},K\}}\sum_{q=0}^{K}\sum_{Z^{\prime}_{q}\subset I_{\pm}}\mathbf{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}|X\cap I_{\pm}|=p,\\
|Z^{\prime}_{q}|=q\end{subarray}}\,b_{\epsilon}\Big([X\cap I_{\pm}],Z^{\prime}_{q},t\Big)v_{n-p+q}^{\epsilon}\Big(X\setminus[X\cap I_{\pm}]\cup Z^{\prime}_{q},t\Big)

(4.6)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+{\sum_{l=1}^{K}\mathbf{1}_{X=I_{\pm}^{l}}b_{\epsilon}\Big({I_{\pm}^{l}},\emptyset,t\Big)}

with

I^{l}_{+}=\{N-(l-1),\dots,N\}\;\textrm{ and }\;I^{l}_{-}=\{-N,\dots,-N+(l-1)\},\quad 1\leq l\leq K,

(4.7)

being the last
l
sites in the right/left boundary, and
the coefficients
b_{\epsilon}(Z,Z^{\prime},t)
, satisfy the following properties:

\displaystyle b_{\epsilon}(\emptyset,Z^{\prime},t)=0,\;\;\;b_{\epsilon}(Z,\emptyset,t)=0\,\;{\rm if}\,|Z|=1

(4.8)

\displaystyle\text{for any integer $M$:}\,\,\sup_{t,|Z|\leq M,|Z^{\prime}|\leq M}|b_{\epsilon}(Z,Z^{\prime},t)|<\infty.

We observe that the last term in (
4.6
), despite showing a sum, it is in fact a unique non zero term when the set
X
matches one of the
I_{\pm}^{l}
for some
l\in{\{1,\dots,K\}}
. We display this term separately from the others since it will be treated with a different strategy. Observe that in (
4.6
),
p=|X\cap I_{\pm}|\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, represents the number of particles that go to the boundary and die, and
q=|Z^{\prime}_{q}|\in\{0,\dots,K\}
, represents the number of particles that are born at the boundary.

4.0.2
Estimating via Duhamel’s formula

Given the evolution equation for the space
v
-function we use the stirring process defined in Section
C
. For
X,Y\subset\Lambda_{N}
with
|X|=|Y|
we use the notation
\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(X\overset{\lambda}{\rightarrow}Y)=\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(X(\lambda)=Y|X(0)=X)
, where
\lambda\geq 0
is a fixed time. In particular, if
X=\{x\}
,
Y=\{y\}
then
\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(X\overset{\lambda}{\rightarrow}Y)=P^{(\epsilon)}_{\lambda}(x,y)
as in (
B.1
). While in
[
6
]
the integral form of the solution of (
4.3
) is given by the initial condition starting at time 0, i.e.,
v_{n}^{\epsilon}(X,0)=0
, here we present the integral form for any starting time
0\leq t^{*}<t
. We denote by
v_{n}^{\epsilon}(X,t;t^{*})
the space
v
-function starting from time
t^{*}
,
and by
v_{n}^{\epsilon}(X,t)
the one starting from
t=0
. We order arbitrarily the sites of
X
as
\underline{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
(which is a labeled configuration now, see definition in Appendix
C
) and set
v_{n}^{\epsilon}(\underline{x},t):=v_{n}^{\epsilon}(X,t)
or
v_{n}^{\epsilon}(\underline{x},t;t^{*}):=v_{n}^{\epsilon}(X,t,t^{*})
. In turn,
{\bf E}_{\epsilon,X}
is written as
{\bf E}_{\epsilon,\underline{x}}
. By the evolution equation (
4.3
) and Duhamel’s formula, we get

v_{n}^{\epsilon}(\underline{x},t;t^{*})={\bf E}_{\epsilon,\underline{x}}\left[v_{n}^{\epsilon}(\underline{x}(t-t^{*}),t^{*})\right]+\int_{t^{*}}^{t}\,d\lambda{\bf E}_{\epsilon,\underline{x}}\left[(C_{\epsilon}v_{n}^{\epsilon})(\underline{x}(\lambda),t-\lambda)\right]

(4.9)

which, by taking into account all the possible states for the stirring process, is the same as

v_{n}^{\epsilon}(\underline{x},t;t^{*})=\sum_{\underline{y}\subset\Lambda_{N}^{\neq,n}}\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(\underline{x}\overset{t-t^{*}}{\rightarrow}\underline{y})v_{n}^{\epsilon}(\underline{y},t^{*})+\int_{t^{*}}^{t}\,d\lambda\sum_{\underline{y}\subset\Lambda_{N}^{\neq,n}}\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(\underline{x}\overset{\lambda}{\rightarrow}\underline{y})(C_{\epsilon}v_{n}^{\epsilon})(\underline{y},t-\lambda).

(4.10)

Recall that
\Lambda_{N}^{\neq,n}
denotes the set of all sequences
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\Lambda_{N}^{n}
with
x_{i}\neq x_{j}
for all
i\neq j
, i.e., all coordinates of
\underline{y}
are distinct. For simplicity of notation, in all subsequent summations we will write
\sum_{\underline{y}}
, which is to be understood as
\sum_{\underline{y}\subset\Lambda_{N}^{\neq,n}}
. We also note that the dependence on
\underline{x}
in
{\bf E}_{\epsilon,\underline{x}}
will be omitted whenever it is clear from the context. Similarly, we omit
t^{*}
from the notation
v_{n}^{\epsilon}(\underline{x},t;t^{*})
and write
v_{n}^{\epsilon}(\underline{x},t)
whenever no confusion arises.

Remark 4.1
.

After computing (
4.2
), we observe that when
n\leq K
and
X
coincide with
\{N-(l-1),\dots,N\}
(or
\{-N,\dots,-N+(l-1)\}
), i.e., when all particles reach the last
l
sites to the right in the boundary and die, one of the possible scenarios is that no new particles are born. This occurs when
l=n
and
X=I^{l}_{\pm}=I^{n}_{\pm}
(and thus
X\setminus[X\cap I_{\pm}]=\emptyset
) and
Z_{q}^{\prime}=\emptyset
(and thus
q=0
in which case the term simplifies to
b\Big(I^{l}_{\pm},\emptyset,t\Big)
since
v_{l-p+q}^{\epsilon}\Big(X\setminus I^{l}_{\pm}\cup\emptyset,t\Big)=v_{l-l+0}^{\epsilon}\Big(\emptyset,t\Big)=1
by (
2.30
). This scenario is represented by
\mathbf{1}_{X=I_{\pm}^{l}}b_{\epsilon}\Big(I_{\pm}^{l},\emptyset,t\Big)

and is exactly the second term in (
4.6
).

Remark 4.2

(
Interpretation of the operator
C_{\epsilon}v_{n}^{\epsilon}
).

The operator
(C_{\epsilon}v_{n}^{\epsilon})
describes how particles move within the system. Specifically,
Av_{n}^{\epsilon}
represents two different scenarios described in the operators
A^{1}v_{n}^{\epsilon}
and
A^{2}v_{n}^{\epsilon}
: either two particles meet - this is indicated by the indicator function
\mathbf{1}_{|x-y|=1}
- and either
x
or
y
dies, or both
\{x,y\}
die, and this is represented by
v^{\epsilon}_{n-1}(X\setminus x,t)
or
v^{\epsilon}_{n-1}(X\setminus y,t)
or
v^{\epsilon}_{n-2}(X\setminus(x\cup y),t)
, respectively. The operator
B_{\pm}v_{n}^{\epsilon}
accounts for cases where one or more particles reach the boundary
I_{\pm}
, i.e., the particles in
X\cap I_{\pm}=Z_{p}
, die, and possibly result in the birth of 0 or more particles, i.e., the particles in
Z_{q}^{\prime}
. This is described by
v_{n-p+q}\left(X\setminus[X\cap I_{\pm}]\cup Z_{q}^{\prime},t\right)
subject to the constraints (
4.8
).

Remark 4.3

(
Delicate terms/scenarios in
C_{\epsilon}v_{n}^{\epsilon}
).

There are three particularly delicate terms in
C_{\epsilon}v_{n}^{\epsilon}
.
Two of them involve
v_{0}^{\epsilon}(\underline{\emptyset},t)
.
The first such term is discussed in Remark
4.1
and it is given by

\mathbf{1}_{X=I_{\pm}^{n}}b_{\epsilon}\big(I_{\pm}^{n},\emptyset,t\big),\quad{n\geq 2}.

(4.11)

The second appears as the second term in (
4.5
) when
n=2
, which simplifies to

(A^{2}v_{2}^{\epsilon})(x,y,t)=-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{1}_{|x-y|=1}\,\big[\rho_{\epsilon}(t,x)-\rho_{\epsilon}(t,y)\big]^{2}

(4.12)

and corresponds to the event where two particles meet, both die, and leave no remaining particles in the system, i.e.,

v_{2-2}^{\epsilon}(X\setminus(x\cup y),t)=v^{\epsilon}(\emptyset,t)=1
. Finally, the third delicate term is

(A^{1}v_{n}^{\epsilon})(X,t)=\sum_{x,y\in X}\mathbf{1}_{|x-y|=1}\big[\rho_{\epsilon}(t,x)-\rho_{\epsilon}(t,y)\big]\big[v_{n-1}^{\epsilon}(X\setminus x,t)-v_{n-1}^{\epsilon}(X\setminus y,t)\big],\quad n\geq 2.

(4.13)

These terms pose challenges that must be carefully addressed in order to establish the main results
stated in Theorem
2.19
(and Theorem
2.24
later).
In fact, the estimates used in
[
6
]
(see Section 5,
[
6
]
) and also provided in Appendix
C
, are not sufficient to obtain the space and space–time estimates in Theorems
2.19
and
2.24
.
For this reason, we distinguish these three terms in the formulas from the outset
and estimate them differently from
[
4
]
, as shown in Lemma
4.7
. On top of this, the term given in (
4.11
) is handled differently in the context of the space–time
v
estimation, see Lemma
4.65
.

We recall (
4.4
) and we bound the rightmost term in (
4.10
) by the sum of the next two terms

\int_{t^{*}}^{t}\,d\lambda\sum_{\underline{y}\subset\Lambda_{N}}\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(\underline{x}\overset{\lambda}{\rightarrow}\underline{y})\epsilon^{-1}|(B_{\pm}v_{n}^{\epsilon})(\underline{y},s-\lambda)|

(4.14)

and

\int_{t^{*}}^{t}\,d\lambda\sum_{\underline{y}\subset\Lambda_{N}}\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(\underline{x}\overset{\lambda}{\rightarrow}\underline{y})\epsilon^{-2}\left(|(A^{1}v^{\epsilon}_{n})(\underline{y},s-\lambda)|+|(A^{2}v^{\epsilon}_{n})(\underline{y},s-\lambda)|\right).

(4.15)

4.0.3
Estimation of the right-hand side of (
4.10
): the boundary contribution.

Now, we estimate the contribution from the boundary generator
B_{\pm}
. By using Lemma 5.2 in
[
6
]
, it is straightforward to show that: For any
n
there is a constant
c
so that for any
\underline{x}\subset\Lambda_{N}
,
|\underline{x}|=n
, and any
\lambda<t\leq\log\epsilon^{-1}

\displaystyle\sum_{\underline{y}\subset\Lambda_{N}}\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(\underline{x}\overset{\lambda}{\rightarrow}\underline{y})\epsilon^{-1}|(B_{\pm}v_{n}^{\epsilon})(\underline{y},t-\lambda)|\leq\sum_{u=\pm}\sum_{\underline{z}^{\prime}\subset I_{\pm}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\subset\{1,\dots,n\},\\
1\leq p\leq\min\{n,K\}\end{subarray}}\big(1-\mathbf{1}_{q=0,p=1}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt\times{{}_{p}^{\epsilon}(\lambda)}\mathbf{E}_{\epsilon}\Big[\mathbf{1}_{\emptyset\neq\underline{x}^{(J)}(\lambda)\subset I_{u}^{c}}|v_{n-p+q}^{\epsilon}\big(\underline{x}^{(J)}(\lambda)\cup\underline{z}^{\prime},t-\lambda\big)|\Big]+\epsilon^{-1}\mathbf{E}_{\epsilon}\Big[{\bf 1}_{\underline{x}(\lambda)=I_{\pm}^{n}}\Big]

(4.16)

where
\underline{x}^{(J)}
is the configuration obtained by erasing from
\underline{x}
all
x_{j}
,
j\in J
and
p=|J|
and

{}_{p}^{\epsilon}(\lambda)=\frac{\epsilon^{-1}}{[\epsilon^{-2}\lambda]^{p/2}+1}.

(4.17)

Above, we estimate (
4.14
) in the same way as in Lemma 5.2 of
[
6
]
, except for the term
\epsilon^{-1}\mathbf{E}_{\epsilon}\Big[{\bf 1}_{\underline{x}(\lambda)=I_{\pm}^{n}}\Big]
