is considered fixed and
\underline{x}
changes over time through the discrete equation for
v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,r}(\underline{y},\underline{x})
given in (
5.1
). For simplicity—as in (
4.2
)—we express the equation in terms of
v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,r}(Y,X)
, where
X,Y\subset\Lambda_{N}
. This is justified by the fact that
v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,r}(\underline{y},\underline{x})
is symmetric under any permutation of the components of
\underline{x}
and
\underline{y}
. When we later write its Duhamel form, we return to the notation involving labeled configurations.

\hskip-2.84544pt\begin{cases}&\hskip-11.38092pt\partial_{r}v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,r}(Y,X)=\epsilon^{-2}(L_{0}v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,r})(Y,X)+(C_{\epsilon}v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,r})(Y,X),\quad r>s,\\
&\hskip-11.38092pt\displaystyle{v^{\epsilon,n,n}_{s,s}(Y,X)=\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\left[\left(\prod_{y\in Y}\bar{\eta}_{s}(y)\right)^{2}\right]},\quad Y=X,\\
&\hskip-11.38092pt\displaystyle{v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,s}(Y,X)=\sum_{L\subset X\cap Y}\psi(L,s)v^{\epsilon}_{|[X\triangle Y]\cup L|}([X\triangle Y]\cup L,s)},\quad Y\neq X\end{cases}

(5.1)

where

{}_{s}(L,\rho_{\epsilon})=\prod_{z\in L}(1-2\rho_{\epsilon}(z,s))\prod_{\tilde{z}\in X\cap Y\setminus L}\chi(\rho_{\epsilon}(\tilde{z},s)).

(5.2)

The generator
L_{0}
is given in (
2.2
) and
C_{\epsilon}
is defined in (
4.4
). The notation
\sum_{L\subset X\cap Y}
means the sum over all possible subsets of
X\cap Y
and
v^{\epsilon}_{|[X\triangle Y]\cup L|}([X\triangle Y]\cup L,s)
is the space correlation
v
-function with
|[X\triangle Y]\cup L|
number of particles of
[X\triangle Y]\cup L
at the smallest time
s
. The discrete equation (
5.1
) is derived from (
4.2
) adapted to the function
v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,r}
by fixing the time
s
and
Y
. An important property of the space-time
v
-correlation function is that

\displaystyle v^{\epsilon,m,0}_{s,r}(Y,\emptyset)=v^{\epsilon}_{m}(Y,s).

(5.3)

Although (
5.1
) is identical to the discrete equation of the space correlation
v
-estimate (
4.3
), the initial condition when
r=s
differs, resulting in a distinct integral solution:

\displaystyle v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,r}(\underline{y},\underline{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\underline{w}}\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(\underline{x}\overset{r-s}{\rightarrow}\underline{w})v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,s}(\underline{w},\underline{y})

(5.4)

\displaystyle+\int_{s}^{r}d\lambda\,\sum_{\underline{w}}\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(\underline{x}\overset{r-\lambda}{\rightarrow}\underline{w})(C_{\epsilon}v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,\lambda})(\underline{y},\underline{w}).

We further analyze the initial condition by splitting into
\underline{w}\neq\underline{y}
and
\underline{w}=\underline{y}
(when
n=m
) and the integral solution takes the form:

\displaystyle v^{\epsilon,n,n}_{s,r}(\underline{y},\underline{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\underline{w}:\,|\underline{w}\cap\underline{y}|=0}\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(\underline{x}\overset{r-s}{\rightarrow}\underline{w})v_{2n}^{\epsilon}(\underline{w}\cup\underline{y},s)+\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(\underline{x}\overset{r-s}{\rightarrow}\underline{y})v^{\epsilon,n,n}_{s,s}(\underline{y},\underline{y})

(5.5)

\displaystyle+\sum_{l=1}^{n-1}\sum_{\underline{w}:\,|\underline{w}\cap\underline{y}|=l}\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(\underline{x}\overset{r-s}{\rightarrow}\underline{w})\sum_{\underline{l}\subset\underline{w}\cap\underline{y}}{}_{s}(l,\rho_{\epsilon})v^{\epsilon}_{|\underline{w}\triangle\underline{y}\cup\underline{l}|}(\underline{w}\triangle\underline{y}\cup\underline{l},s)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\int_{s}^{r}d\lambda\,\sum_{\underline{w}}\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(\underline{x}\overset{r-\lambda}{\rightarrow}\underline{w})(C_{\epsilon}v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,\lambda})(\underline{y},\underline{w}),

where
v^{\epsilon,n,n}_{s,s}(\underline{y},\underline{y})
is given by the middle equation in (
5.1
), while when
n\neq m
, we write

\displaystyle v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,r}(\underline{x},\underline{y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\underline{w}:\,|\underline{w}\cap\underline{y}|=0}\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(\underline{x}\overset{r-s}{\rightarrow}\underline{w})v_{n+m}^{\epsilon}(\underline{w}\cup\underline{y},s)

(5.6)

\displaystyle+\sum_{l=1}^{\min\{n,m\}}\sum_{\underline{w}:\,|\underline{w}\cap\underline{y}|=l}\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(\underline{x}\overset{r-s}{\rightarrow}\underline{w})\sum_{\underline{l}\subset\underline{w}\cap\underline{y}}{}_{s}(l,\rho_{\epsilon})v^{\epsilon}_{|\underline{w}\triangle\underline{y}\cup\underline{l}|}\left(\left(\underline{w}\triangle\underline{y},\underline{l}\right),s\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\int_{s}^{r}d\lambda\,\sum_{\underline{w}}\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(\underline{x}\overset{r-\lambda}{\rightarrow}\underline{w})(C_{\epsilon}v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,\lambda})(\underline{y},\underline{w}).

For
s<\lambda
, the space-time correlation
v
-function,
v^{\epsilon,m,l}_{s,\lambda}
, is bounded by

\displaystyle|v^{\epsilon,m,l}_{s,\lambda}(\underline{y},\underline{w})|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{m}\bar{\eta}_{s}(y_{j})\prod_{i=1}^{l}\bar{\eta}_{\lambda}(w_{i})\right]\right|

(5.7)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\left[\left|\prod_{j=1}^{m}\bar{\eta}_{s}(y_{j})\right|\;\underbrace{\left|\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{l}\bar{\eta}_{\lambda}(w_{i})|\mathcal{F}(\{\eta_{s^{\prime}}:s^{\prime}\leq s\})\right]\right|}_{:=|v_{l}^{\epsilon}(\underline{w},\lambda;s)|}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\epsilon^{1-2\zeta}{\bf 1}_{l\leq 2}+\epsilon{\bf 1}_{3\leq l\leq K}+\epsilon^{1+\zeta}{\bf 1}_{l\geq K+1},

where we apply Theorem
2.19
to the last inequality.
However, in Theorem
2.24
, we show that this bound can be improved by carefully studying and analyzing (
5.5
)–(
5.6
), as discussed in Sections
5.1
–
5.2
.
The construction of the integral inequality for space-time correlation
v
-function requires the estimation of the initial condition:

\bullet
First line in (
5.5
) and (
5.6
):

\sum_{\underline{w}:\,|\underline{w}\cap\underline{y}|=0}\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(\underline{x}\overset{r-s}{\rightarrow}\underline{w})v_{n+m}^{\epsilon}(\underline{w}\cup\underline{y},s)+\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(\underline{x}\overset{r-s}{\rightarrow}\underline{y})v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,s}(\underline{y},\underline{y})\mathbf{1}_{n=m}.

In fact, from Corollary
4.14
and Lemma
4.15
, we have that for
\zeta>0
, there exists a positive integer
N^{*}
(large enough) such that
\sup_{0<t\leq T}\sup_{\underline{x}\in\Lambda_{N}^{n,\neq}}|v^{\epsilon}_{n}(\underline{x},t)|\leq c\epsilon^{1+\zeta}
,
n\geq N^{*}
. For
n<N^{*}
, we obtain

\sum_{\underline{w}:\,|\underline{w}\cap\underline{y}|=0}\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(\underline{x}\overset{r-s}{\rightarrow}W)v_{n+m}^{\epsilon}(\underline{w}\cup\underline{y},s)\lesssim D^{\epsilon}_{n+m}(r-s),

(5.8)

followed by Lemma
4.15
because

\sum_{\underline{w}:\,|\underline{w}\cap\underline{y}|=0}\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(\underline{x}\overset{r-s}{\rightarrow}\underline{w})v_{n+m}^{\epsilon}(\underline{w}\cup\underline{y},s)\leq\sum_{\pi\in S_{n}}\sum_{{\begin{subarray}{c}\underline{w}=(w_{1},\dots,w_{n}):\\
|\underline{w}\cap\underline{y}|=0\end{subarray}}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}P^{(\epsilon)}_{\lambda}(x_{\pi(i)},w_{i})\,|v_{n+m}^{\epsilon}(\underline{w}\cup\underline{y},s)|,

after applying Lemma
C.7
and Remark
C.8
.
For the second term,

\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(\underline{x}\overset{r-s}{\rightarrow}\underline{y})|v^{\epsilon,n,n}_{s,s}(\underline{y},\underline{y})|\lesssim\begin{cases}\frac{1}{{[\epsilon^{-2}(r-s)]}^{1/2}+1},&n=1\\
\frac{1}{{[\epsilon^{-2}(r-s)]}^{n/2}+1},&n\geq 2\end{cases}

(5.9)

where we use Lemma
C.7
and the fact that
|v^{\epsilon,n,n}_{s,s}(\underline{y},\underline{y})|\leq 1
. When
n=m=1
we define:

C_{1,1}^{\epsilon}(r-s):=\frac{\epsilon}{{(r-s)}^{1/2}}.

(5.10)

\bullet
When
n=m\geq 2
, the second line in (
5.5
) can be bounded as

\frac{1}{{[\epsilon^{-2}(r-s)]}^{n/2}+1}+\sum_{l=1}^{n-1}\frac{\epsilon^{1-2\zeta}}{{[\epsilon^{-2}(r-s)]}^{l/2}+1}\lesssim\frac{\epsilon^{2-2\zeta}}{{(r-s)}^{1-\zeta}}+\frac{\epsilon^{2-2\zeta}}{{(r-s)}^{1/2}}=:C_{n,n}^{\epsilon}(r-s).

(5.11)

\bullet
When
n\neq m
, the second line in (
5.6
) can be treated as

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{\min\{n,m\}}\sum_{\underline{w}:\,|\underline{w}\cap\underline{w}|=l}\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(\underline{x}\overset{r-s}{\rightarrow}\underline{w})\sum_{\underline{l}\subset\underline{w}\cap\underline{y}}{}_{s}(\underline{l},\rho_{\epsilon})v^{\epsilon}_{|\underline{w}\triangle\underline{y}\cup\underline{l}|}(\underline{w}\triangle\underline{y}\cup\underline{l},s)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\lesssim\sum_{l=1}^{\min\{n,m\}}\frac{\epsilon^{1-2\zeta}}{{[\epsilon^{-2}(r-s)]}^{l/2}+1}\lesssim\frac{\epsilon^{2-2\zeta}}{{(r-s)}^{1/2}}=:C_{n,m}^{\epsilon}(r-s).

(5.12)

\bullet
Finally, the method for bounding the integral term in (
5.5
)–(
5.6
), i.e.,

\int_{s}^{r}d\lambda\,\sum_{\underline{w}}\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(\underline{x}\overset{r-\lambda}{\rightarrow}\underline{w})(C_{\epsilon}v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,\lambda})(\underline{y},\underline{w})

parallels the proof of Lemma
4.11
, as both
(C_{\epsilon}v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,r-s-\lambda})
and
(C_{\epsilon}v^{\epsilon}_{n})
involve the same operator. The key distinction arises in the treatment of scenarios where all particles die and no particles are left in the system. In such cases,
(C_{\epsilon}v^{\epsilon}_{n})
includes
v_{0}(\emptyset,t-\lambda)=1
(see Definition
2.15
) while
(C_{\epsilon}v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,r-s-\lambda})
includes
v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,r-s-\lambda}(\underline{y},\emptyset)=v^{\epsilon}_{m}(\underline{y},s)
, see (
5.3
). This allows to improve the estimation of these terms by using Theorem
2.19
for
v^{\epsilon}_{m}(\underline{y},s)
. The precise estimation of the integral term is given in the proof of Lemma
5.1
.

5.1
Integral inequalities for space-time
v
-functions

In this section, we present the integral probabilities for the space-time
v
-function. For
s<r
, we denote

a_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,r)=\sup_{\underline{x}\in\Lambda_{N}^{n,\neq}}\sup_{\underline{y}\in\Lambda_{N}^{m,\neq}}|v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,r}(\underline{y},\underline{x})|,\;\;\;\;a_{\epsilon,s}(0,m,r)=|v_{m}^{\epsilon}(\underline{y},r)|

d_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,r)=\sup_{\underline{x}\in\Lambda_{N}^{n,\neq}}\sup_{\underline{y}\in\Lambda_{N}^{m,\neq}}|v_{s,r}^{\epsilon,m,n}(\underline{y},\underline{x})-v_{s,r}^{\epsilon,m,n}(\underline{y},\underline{x}+\underline{e}_{1})|,\;\;\;\;d_{\epsilon,s}(0,m,r)=0.

(5.13)

We make use of the bound (
5.7
) in the case
n\geq K+1
, i.e.,

|v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,\lambda}(\underline{y},\underline{w})|\leq C\epsilon^{1+\zeta},\;\;m\geq 1.

When applied to the cases
n=1,2
, these properties yield an estimate of order
\epsilon^{1-2\zeta}
, which is insufficient for our purposes, particularly in showing that terms in (
3.10
)–(
3.15
) are vanishing and in proving tightness. To obtain a sharper estimate of order
\epsilon
and
\epsilon^{1+\zeta}
, we adopt a different approach, beginning with the integral inequalities.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
K\geq 2
,
0\leq s<r\leq T
and
\zeta>0
.
For
n,m\geq 1
, the following integral inequalities for
a_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,r)
hold:

\displaystyle a_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,r)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(C^{\epsilon}_{n,n}(r-s)+D^{\epsilon}_{n+n}(r-s)\right){\bf 1}_{n=m}

(5.14)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\left(C_{n,m}^{\epsilon}(r-s)+D^{\epsilon}_{n+m}(r-s)\right){\bf 1}_{n\neq m}+\epsilon^{1+\zeta}(r-s)^{\zeta}\mathbf{1}_{n\geq 2,m\geq 1}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+{\bf 1}_{n\geq 2}\int_{s}^{r}d\lambda\left(\frac{1}{(r-\lambda)^{1/2}}+\frac{1}{(r-\lambda)^{1/2+\zeta}}\right)d_{\epsilon,s}(n-1,m,\lambda)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\mathbf{1}_{n\geq 1}\int_{s}^{r}d\lambda\frac{\epsilon^{-1}}{[\epsilon^{-2}(r-\lambda)]^{1/2}}\sum_{j=n}^{n-1+K}a_{\epsilon,s}(j,m,\lambda),

where
C_{n,m}^{\epsilon}(,r-s),C_{n,m}^{\epsilon}(,r-s)
and
D^{\epsilon}_{n+m}(r-s)
are given in (
5.10
)–(
5
), and (
4.66
), respectively.

Proof.

As previously noted, since the discrete equations satisfied by

v_{n}^{\epsilon}(\underline{x},r)
and

v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,r}(\underline{y},\underline{x})
involve the same operator,
the integral term in Duhamel’s formula for
v^{\epsilon,m,n}_{s,r}
coincides with that of

v_{n}^{\epsilon}
. Consequently, the integral terms appearing in both
(
5.5
) and
(
5.6
) can be estimated in the same way as in
Lemma
4.11
, with the only difference that the 2nd and the last term on the right-hand side of (
4.44
) are estimated directly using (
5.7
),
i.e.,

\int_{s}^{r}d\lambda\;\frac{\epsilon^{-1}}{[\epsilon^{-2}\lambda]^{1-\zeta}}\sum_{q=0}^{K}\;\;\;\sum_{p=2}^{\min\{n,K\}}a_{\epsilon,s}(n-p+q,t-\lambda)\lesssim\epsilon^{1-2\zeta}\epsilon^{1-2\zeta}(r-s)^{\zeta}\leq\epsilon^{1+\zeta}(r-s)^{\zeta}

and for
n\geq 2

\int_{s}^{r}d\lambda\epsilon\left(\frac{1}{(r-\lambda)^{1/2}}+\frac{1}{(r-\lambda)^{3/4}}\right)a_{\epsilon,s}(n-2,m,\lambda)\lesssim\epsilon\epsilon^{1-2\zeta}\left((r-s)^{1/2}+(r-s)^{1/4}\right)\leq\epsilon^{1+\zeta}(r-s)^{\zeta}.

Finally, by using property (
5.3
),

\int_{s}^{r}d\lambda\;\epsilon^{-1}\mathbf{E}_{\epsilon}\left[{\bf 1}_{\underline{x}((r-\lambda))=I^{n}_{+}}a_{\epsilon,s}(0,m,\lambda)\right]\lesssim\epsilon^{1+\zeta}(r-s)^{\zeta}.

Finally, we write the second term of the right-hand side in (
4.44
) (but with
a_{\epsilon,s}(n-1+q,m,\lambda)
in place of
a_{\epsilon}(n-1+q,m,\lambda)
) as
\sum_{q=1}^{K}a_{\epsilon,s}(n-1+q,m,\lambda)=\sum_{j=n}^{n-1+K}a_{\epsilon,s}(j,m,\lambda)
.
∎

Similarly to Lemma
5.1
, we obtain the integral inequality for the difference of the space-time
v
-functions as stated in the next Lemma.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
K\geq 2
,
0\leq s<r\leq T
and
\zeta>0
.
the following integral inequalities for
d_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,r)
hold:

\displaystyle d_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,r)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{n=m=1}\epsilon^{2-2\zeta}\left(\frac{1}{(r-s)^{1/2}}+\frac{1}{(r-s)^{1-\zeta}}\right)

(5.15)

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt+\mathbf{1}_{n+m\geq 3}\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2-2\zeta}}{(r-s)^{1/2}}+C^{\epsilon}_{n,n}(r-s){\bf 1}_{n=m}+C_{n,m}^{\epsilon}(r-s){\bf 1}_{n\neq m}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt+\mathbf{1}_{n+m\geq 2}\epsilon^{1+\zeta}(r-s)^{\zeta}

where
C_{n,m}^{\epsilon}(,r-s),C_{n,m}^{\epsilon}(,r-s)
are given in (
5.10
)–(
5
) respectively.

Proof.

The difference of the Duhamel’s formulas of space-time
v
- functions, and in turn for
d_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,r)
, is estimated as in (
4.12
) along with the initial condition, namely,

\displaystyle d_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,r)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon^{2-2\zeta}}{(r-s)^{1/2}}+\mathbf{1}_{n+m\geq 3}\left(C^{\epsilon}_{n,n}(r-s){\bf 1}_{n=m}+C_{n,m}^{\epsilon}(,r-s){\bf 1}_{n\neq m}\right)

(5.16)

\displaystyle+\mathbf{1}_{2\leq n\leq K}\epsilon^{1+\zeta}(r-s)^{\zeta}

\displaystyle+\int_{s}^{r}d\lambda\;\frac{\epsilon^{-1}}{[\epsilon^{-2}\lambda]^{1-\zeta}}\sum_{q=1}^{K}a_{\epsilon,s}(n-1+q,m,r-\lambda)

\displaystyle+\mathbf{1}_{n\geq 2}\int_{s}^{r}d\lambda\frac{\epsilon^{3/2-2\zeta}}{\lambda^{1-\zeta}}a_{\epsilon,s}(n-2,m,r-\lambda)

\displaystyle+\mathbf{1}_{n\geq 2}\int_{s}^{r}d\lambda\frac{\epsilon^{1-3\zeta}}{\lambda^{1-\zeta}}d_{\epsilon,s}(n-1,m,r-\lambda).

The first term in (
5.16
) follows from the estimation of the initial condition of
|v_{s,r}^{\epsilon,n,m}(\underline{y},\underline{x})-v_{s,r}^{\epsilon,n,m}(\underline{y},\underline{x}+\underline{e}_{1})|
. For
n=m=1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\epsilon,x}\left[v_{s,r}^{\epsilon,1,1}(y,x(r-s)\right]-\mathbf{E}_{\epsilon,x+1}\left[v_{s,r}^{\epsilon,1,1}(y,x(r-s))\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\sum_{w:\,w\neq y}\left(\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(x\overset{r-s}{\rightarrow}w)-\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(x+1\overset{r-s}{\rightarrow}w)\right)v_{2}^{\epsilon}(w,y,s)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\left(\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(x\overset{r-s}{\rightarrow}y)-\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(x+1\overset{r-s}{\rightarrow}y)\right)v^{\epsilon,1,1}_{s,s}(y,y)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\lesssim\frac{1}{[\epsilon^{-2}(r-s)]^{1/2}+1}\epsilon^{1-2\zeta}+\frac{1}{[\epsilon^{-2}(r-s)]+1}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\lesssim\frac{\epsilon^{2-2\zeta}}{(r-s)^{1/2}}+\frac{\epsilon^{2-2\zeta}}{(r-s)^{1-\zeta}},

where the first inequality above is obtained as follows

\displaystyle\sum_{w:\,w\neq y}\left(\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(x\overset{r-s}{\rightarrow}w)-\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(x+1\overset{r-s}{\rightarrow}w)\right)v_{2}^{\epsilon}(w,y,s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{w:\,w\neq y}\left(P^{(\epsilon)}_{r-s}(x,w)-P^{(\epsilon)}_{r-s}(x+1,w)\right)v_{2}^{\epsilon}(w,y,s)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{w:\,w\neq y}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon^{-2}(r-s)}+1}G_{\epsilon^{-2}(r-s)}(x,w)|v_{2}^{\epsilon}(w,y,s)|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon^{-2}(r-s)}+1}\epsilon^{1-2\zeta}\underbrace{\sum_{w:\,w\neq y}G_{\epsilon^{-2}(r-s)}(x,w)}_{\leq 1}\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon^{-2}(r-s)}+1}\epsilon^{1-2\zeta}

(5.17)

For the other term

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(x\overset{r-s}{\rightarrow}y)-\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(x+1\overset{r-s}{\rightarrow}y)||v^{\epsilon,1,1}_{s,s}(y,y)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|P^{(\epsilon)}_{r-s}(x,y)-P^{(\epsilon)}_{r-s}(x+1,y)||v^{\epsilon,1,1}_{s,s}(y,y)|

(5.18)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon^{-2}(r-s)}+1}\underbrace{G_{\epsilon^{-2}(r-s)}(x,w)}_{\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon^{-2}(r-s)}+1}}\underbrace{|v^{\epsilon,1,1}_{s,s}(y,y)|}_{\leq 1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\epsilon^{-2}(r-s)+1}

When
n+m\geq 3
, we estimate the difference

\mathbf{E}_{\epsilon,\underline{x}}\left[|v_{s,r}^{\epsilon,m,n}(\underline{y},\underline{x}(r-s))\right]-\mathbf{E}_{\epsilon,\underline{x}+\underline{e}_{1}}\left[v_{s,r}^{\epsilon,m,n}(\underline{y},\underline{x}(r-s))|\right],

by enlarging the space, resulting in
\underline{\tilde{x}}=\left(x_{1},x_{1}+1,x_{2},x_{3},\dots,x_{n}\right)
(and thus
|\underline{\tilde{x}}|=n+1
) and applying the same argument used to obtain the initial condition in (
4.22
):

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\epsilon,\underline{\tilde{x}}}\left[\left(v_{s,r}^{\epsilon,n,m}(\underline{y},\underline{\tilde{x}}^{(2)}(r-s))-v_{s,r}^{\epsilon,n,m}(\underline{y},\underline{\tilde{x}}^{(1)}(r-s))\right)\left(\mathbf{1}_{|\underline{\tilde{x}}(r-s)\cap\underline{y}|=0}+\mathbf{1}_{|\underline{\tilde{x}}(r-s)\cap\underline{y}|\neq 0}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}\left(\tau_{1,2}>\frac{r-s}{2}\right)a_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,s)+C^{\epsilon}_{n,m}(r-s)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\epsilon^{2-2\zeta}}{(r-s)^{1/2}}+C^{\epsilon}_{n,m}(r-s),

where
C^{\epsilon}_{n,m}(r-s)
is given in (
5.11
)–(
5
). The last inequality comes from the fact that

\left(v_{s,r}^{\epsilon,n,m}(\underline{y},\underline{\tilde{x}}^{(2)}(r-s))-v_{s,r}^{\epsilon,n,m}(\underline{y},\underline{\tilde{x}}^{\prime(1)}(r-s))\right)\mathbf{1}_{|\underline{\tilde{x}}(r-s)\cap\underline{y}|=0}

is an antisymmetric function and thus Lemma
C.4
applies. The estimation of this term is similar to the first term in (
4.22
). To estimate
a_{\epsilon,s}
, we use Theorem
2.19
, leading to
\epsilon^{2-2\zeta}
. By using (
5.16
), (
5.7
) and property (
5.3
), we obtain the integral inequality.
∎

5.2
Proof of Theorem
2.24

The proof follows the same steps as in the proof of Theorem
2.19
.
The difference lies in the fact that, in this case, we must also control the initial conditions,
namely
C_{n,n}^{\epsilon}(r-s)
,
C_{n,m}^{\epsilon}(r-s)
, and
D^{\epsilon}_{n+m}(r-s)
,
as given in (
5.10
)–(
5
) and in Lemma
4.15
, respectively. Specifically, given a time
s>0
, we choose
\tilde{\Delta}<1
and show that when
n+m=2,3

\sup_{s\leq\sigma\leq s+\tilde{\Delta}}\left\{(\sigma-s)^{1/2}a_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,\sigma)\right\}\lesssim\epsilon

and for
n+m\geq 4

\sup_{s\leq\sigma\leq s+\tilde{\Delta}}\left\{(\sigma-s)^{1-\zeta}a_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,\sigma)\right\}\lesssim\epsilon^{1+\zeta}.

This directly implies that for any time
r>0
such that
r-s\leq\tilde{\Delta}
, the following estimate holds: for
n+m=2,3

(r-s)^{1/2}a_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,r)\leq\sup_{s\leq\sigma\leq s+\tilde{\Delta}}\left\{(\sigma-s)^{1/2}a_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,\sigma)\right\}\lesssim\epsilon,

which gives us
a_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,r)\leq\frac{\epsilon}{(r-s)^{1/2}}

and for
n+m\geq 4

(r-s)^{1-\zeta}a_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,r)\leq\sup_{s\leq\sigma\leq s+\tilde{\Delta}}\left\{(\sigma-s)^{1-\zeta}a_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,\sigma)\right\}\lesssim\epsilon^{1+\zeta},

which in turn gives us
a_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,r)\leq\frac{\epsilon^{1+\zeta}}{(r-s)^{1-\zeta}}
.
The above estimate is desirable in the sense that it depends on the time difference
r-s
, which may be extremely small, on the order of
\epsilon
or even smaller. Consequently, the factors
(r-s)^{-1/2}
or
(r-s)^{-(1-\zeta)}
can become very large, and substituting their orders directly may lead to an overall excessively large bound. Therefore, when
s
and
r
are close, the estimate is expected to depend explicitly on the time difference
r-s
.
On the other hand, when
r>s+\tilde{\Delta}
,
s,r
are not close anymore and the bound can be independent of
r-s
. In fact, we prove that when
r>s+\tilde{\Delta}
,

a_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,r)\lesssim\epsilon
,
n+m=2,3
and
a_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,r)\lesssim\epsilon^{1+\zeta}
,
n+m\geq 4
.

We start with the first case: Since
0<\tilde{\Delta}<1
, the initial conditions can be simplified and bounded in a unified way as follows:

\displaystyle C_{n,m}(\sigma-s)+D^{\epsilon}_{n+m}(\sigma-s)

\displaystyle\lesssim\epsilon\frac{1}{(\sigma-s)^{1/2}},\qquad n+m=2,3

\displaystyle C_{n,n}(\sigma-s)+D^{\epsilon}_{n+n}(\sigma-s)

\displaystyle\lesssim\epsilon^{1+\zeta}\frac{1}{(\sigma-s)^{1-\zeta}},\qquad n+m\geq 4.

(5.19)

The terms
\frac{\epsilon^{2-2\zeta}}{(\sigma-s)^{1/2}}
and
\epsilon^{2-2\zeta}\left(\frac{1}{(\sigma-s)^{1/2}}+\frac{1}{(\sigma-s)^{1-\zeta}}\right)
in (
5.15
) are bounded by
\frac{\epsilon^{1+\zeta}}{(\sigma-s)^{1/2}}
and
\frac{\epsilon^{1+\zeta}}{(\sigma-s)^{1-\zeta}}
. Next, we define

\displaystyle a^{*}_{\epsilon,s}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\sup_{s\leq\sigma\leq s+\tilde{\Delta}}\tilde{\Gamma}_{a}(n,m,\sigma-s)a_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,\sigma)

\displaystyle d^{*}_{\epsilon,s}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\sup_{s\leq\sigma\leq s+\tilde{\Delta}}\tilde{\Gamma}_{d}(n,m,\sigma-s)d_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,\sigma),

where similarly to the proof of Theorem
2.19
, we denoted

\displaystyle\tilde{\Gamma}_{a}(n,m,\sigma-s)

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{-1}(\sigma-s)^{1/2}\mathbf{1}_{n+m=2,3}+\epsilon^{-1-\zeta}(\sigma-s)^{1-\zeta}\mathbf{1}_{n+m\geq 4}

\displaystyle\tilde{\Gamma}_{d}(n,m,\sigma-s)

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{-1-\zeta}\Big\{(\sigma-s)^{1-\zeta}\Big(\mathbf{1}_{n=m=1}+\mathbf{1}_{n\geq 2,m\geq 1}\Big)+(\sigma-s)^{1/2}\mathbf{1}_{n=1,m\geq 2}\Big\}.

We also need to introduce:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{a}(n,m,\sigma-s)

\displaystyle=\epsilon(\sigma-s)^{-1/2}\mathbf{1}_{n+m=2,3}+\epsilon^{1+\zeta}(\sigma-s)^{-1+\zeta}\mathbf{1}_{n+m\geq 4}

\displaystyle\Gamma_{d}(n,m,\sigma-s)

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{1+\zeta}\Big\{(\sigma-s)^{-1+\zeta}\left(\mathbf{1}_{n=m=1}+\mathbf{1}_{n\geq 2,m\geq 1}\right)+(\sigma-s)^{-1/2}\mathbf{1}_{n=1,m\geq 2}\Big\}.

Starting from the integral inequality in Lemma
5.1
, we first build
a^{*}_{\epsilon,s}(n,m)
and
d^{*}_{\epsilon,s}(n-1,m)
on the right-hand side of (
5.14
) by multiplying with
\Gamma_{a}(j,m,\lambda-s)

and
\tilde{\Gamma}_{a}(j,m,\lambda-s)
the terms including
a_{\epsilon,s}
, as well as,
\Gamma_{d}(n-1,m,\lambda-s)

and
\tilde{\Gamma}_{d}(n-1,m,\lambda-s)
the terms including
d_{\epsilon,s}
. Then, we
multiply both sides of (
5.14
) by the corresponding
\tilde{\Gamma}_{a}(n,m,\sigma-s)
, i.e,

\displaystyle\tilde{\Gamma}_{a}(n,m,\sigma-s)a_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,\sigma)\lesssim\tilde{\Gamma}_{a}(n,m,\sigma-s)\left(\frac{\epsilon}{(\sigma-s)^{1/2}}{\bf 1}_{n+m=2,3}+\frac{\epsilon^{1+\zeta}}{(\sigma-s)^{1-\zeta}}{\bf 1}_{n+m\geq 4}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\epsilon^{1+\zeta}(\sigma-s)^{\zeta}\tilde{\Gamma}_{a}(n,m,\sigma-s)\mathbf{1}_{n\geq 2,m\geq 1}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+{\bf 1}_{n\geq 2}d^{*}_{\epsilon,s}(n-1,m)\tilde{\Gamma}_{a}(n,m,\sigma-s)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\times\int_{s}^{\sigma}d\lambda\left(\frac{1}{(\sigma-\lambda)^{1/2}}+\frac{1}{(\sigma-\lambda)^{1/2+\zeta}}\right)\Gamma_{d}(n-1,m,\lambda-s)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\mathbf{1}_{n\geq 1}\sum_{j=n}^{n-1+K}a^{*}_{\epsilon,s}(j,m)\tilde{\Gamma}_{a}(n,m,\sigma-s)\times\int_{s}^{\sigma}d\lambda\frac{\epsilon^{-1}}{[\epsilon^{-2}(\sigma-\lambda)]^{1/2}}\Gamma_{a}(j,m,\lambda-s),

(5.20)

where we have used (
5.2
) in the first term of the right-hand side above. By integrating the above integral terms, and then considering
\,\sup_{s\leq\sigma\leq s+\Delta}
, we obtain the inequality:

\displaystyle a^{*}_{\epsilon,s}(n,m)

\displaystyle\lesssim({\bf 1}_{n+m=2,3}+{\bf 1}_{n+m\geq 4})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\epsilon^{\zeta}{\bf 1}_{n+m=2,3}+\tilde{\Delta}{\bf 1}_{n+m\geq 4}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\left(\epsilon^{\zeta}\mathbf{1}_{n=2,m=1}+\mathbf{1}_{n\geq 3,m\geq 1}\right)d^{*}_{\epsilon,s}(n-1,m)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\mathbf{1}_{n\geq 1}\tilde{\Delta}^{\zeta}\sum_{q=1}^{K}a^{*}_{\epsilon,s}(n-1+q,m).

The supremum of the first term on the right-hand side of (
5.2
) gives us the first term on the right-hand side of (
5.2
), i.e.,

\sup_{s\leq\sigma\leq s+\tilde{\Delta}}\tilde{\Gamma}_{a}(n,m,\sigma-s)\left(\frac{\epsilon}{(\sigma-s)^{1/2}}{\bf 1}_{n+m=2,3}+\frac{\epsilon^{1+\zeta}}{(\sigma-s)^{1-\zeta}}{\bf 1}_{n+m\geq 4}\right)=({\bf 1}_{n+m=2,3}+{\bf 1}_{n+m\geq 4}).

The second term on the right-hand side of (
5.2
) follows from the same computation, after performing similar steps as above. The third term on the right-hand side of (
5.2
) is obtained by

\displaystyle\sup_{s\leq\sigma\leq s+\tilde{\Delta}}\left\{\tilde{\Gamma}_{a}(n,m,\sigma-s)\int_{s}^{\sigma}d\lambda\frac{1}{(\sigma-\lambda)^{1/2+\zeta}}\Gamma_{d}(n-1,m,\lambda-s)\right\}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sup_{s\leq\sigma\leq s+\tilde{\Delta}}\{(\sigma-s)^{1/2-\zeta}(\sigma-s)^{-1/2+\zeta}\mathbf{1}_{n=2,m=1}\}+\mathbf{1}_{n\geq 3,m\geq 1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbf{1}_{n=2,m=1}+\mathbf{1}_{n\geq 3,m\geq 1},

where we integrated using the formula
\int_{u}^{v}\frac{1}{(s-u)^{a}(v-s)^{b}}ds=c_{a,b}(v-u)^{1-a-b}
where
c_{a,b}=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{s^{a}(1-s)^{b}}ds<\infty
. For example, when
n-1\geq 2,m\geq 1
:

\tilde{\Gamma}_{a}(n,m,\sigma-s)\int_{s}^{\sigma}d\lambda\frac{1}{(\sigma-\lambda)^{1/2+\zeta}}\frac{\epsilon^{1+\zeta}}{(\lambda-s)^{1-\zeta}}\lesssim\epsilon^{-1-\zeta}(\sigma-s)^{1/2}\epsilon^{1+\zeta}(\sigma-s)^{-1/2}=1.

The fourth line follows a similar calculation as above. Similarly, starting from (
5.15
), we obtain

\displaystyle d^{*}_{\epsilon,s}(n,m)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sup_{s\leq\sigma\leq s+\Delta}\Bigg\{\tilde{\Gamma}_{d}(n,m,\sigma-s)\Big(\frac{\epsilon^{2-2\zeta}}{(r-s)^{1/2}}+C^{\epsilon}_{n,n}(r-s){\bf 1}_{n=m}+C_{n,m}^{\epsilon}(,r-s){\bf 1}_{n\neq m}

(5.21)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\mathbf{1}_{n+m\geq 2}\epsilon^{1+\zeta}(\sigma-s)^{\zeta}\Big)\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle 1+\mathbf{1}_{n+m\geq 2}\,\epsilon^{\zeta}\Delta^{1/2+\zeta}.

We recall the positive integer
N^{*}
given in (
2.33
), i.e.,

(2-\beta^{*})c^{*}(N^{*}+1)>\zeta+1

and by (
5.7
), we have, for any positive integer
j>N^{*}
that
a^{*}_{\epsilon,s}(j,m)\lesssim\epsilon^{\frac{n}{2}-\zeta}
, an estimate used in all
j\geq N^{*}+1
of
\sum_{j=n}^{n-1+K}a^{*}_{\epsilon,s}(j,m)
.
As in the proof of Theorem
2.19

in Section
4.1
, here for
m\geq 1
we define the vectors

\displaystyle\underline{x}^{(m)}

\displaystyle=[a^{*}_{\epsilon,s}(1,m)\,\cdots a^{*}_{\epsilon,s}(N^{*},m)\,\,d^{*}_{\epsilon,s}(1,m)\,\cdots\,d^{*}_{\epsilon,s}(N^{*},k)\,]^{T}

and

\displaystyle u_{\epsilon,\tilde{\Delta}}^{(m)}=[u_{\epsilon,\tilde{\Delta}}(1,m)\,\cdots\,u_{\epsilon,\tilde{\Delta}}(2N^{*},m)]^{T},

which can be compactly written as

\underline{x}^{(m)}\leq\Lambda^{m}_{\epsilon,\tilde{\Delta}}\,\underline{x}^{(m)}+u_{\epsilon,\tilde{\Delta}}^{(m)}.

The elements of
\Lambda^{(m)}_{\epsilon,\tilde{\Delta}}
are specified in the same way as of
A_{\epsilon,\tilde{\Delta}}
in Section
4.1
.
We choose
\tilde{\Delta}
(which is less than 1) such that

\|\Lambda^{(m)}_{\epsilon,\tilde{\Delta}}\|_{\infty}<1/2

where
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
is the matrix norm defined as the maximum absolute row sum of the matrix. The matrices
\Lambda^{(m)}_{\epsilon,\tilde{\Delta}}
and
u^{(m)}_{\epsilon,\tilde{\Delta}}
are bounded by 1/2 and some
c
uniformly in
\epsilon
respectively. From this, all components of
\underline{x}
are uniformly bounded in
\epsilon
which proves the result.

We proceed to the case where given a time
s>0
,
s+\tilde{\Delta}\leq r\leq T
. This means that
T-s\geq\tilde{\Delta}
, and thus we first split the interval
[s,T]
into
l\in\mdmathbb{Z}
, to be chosen later, equal sub-intervals
[s_{k-1},s_{k}]
with
s_{0}=s
and
s_{l+1}=T
. The length of the each interval is given by
|s_{k}-s_{k-1}|=\Delta
such that
\Delta\leq\tilde{\Delta}
and
\frac{T}{\Delta}=l
. Note that we use the same notation for the length of the sub–intervals, namely
\Delta
, as in the proof of Theorem
2.19
.
Although the two quantities play the same conceptual role, their magnitudes may differ.
We prove that given
m\geq 1

\sup_{s\leq\sigma\leq T}a_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,\sigma)\lesssim\epsilon\textbf{1}_{n+m=2,3}+\epsilon^{1+\zeta}\textbf{1}_{n+m\geq 4},

which implies that
a_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,r)\lesssim\epsilon\textbf{1}_{n+m=2,3}+\epsilon^{1+\zeta}\textbf{1}_{n+m\geq 4}.
For that reason, we define

a_{\epsilon,s}^{*}(n,m,k)=\sup_{s\leq\sigma\leq s+k\Delta}a_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,\sigma)\Big\{\epsilon^{-1}\textbf{1}_{n+m=2,3}+\epsilon^{-1-\zeta}\textbf{1}_{n+m\geq 4}\Big\}

as well as
as well as

d_{\epsilon,s}^{*}(n,m,k)=\epsilon^{-1-\zeta}\sup_{s\leq\sigma\leq s+k\Delta}d_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,\sigma),\qquad n+m\geq 2.

By (
5.4
) starting from
k\Delta
(instead of
s
as is in (
5.4
)) along with the integral term in (
5.14
) and based on the calculations we presented for the previous case, the integral inequality for
a_{\epsilon,s}^{*}(n,m,k)
and
d_{\epsilon,s}^{*}(n,m,k)
take the form: for
k=2,\dots,l+1

\displaystyle a^{*}_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,k)

\displaystyle\lesssim a^{*}_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,k-1)+\epsilon^{\zeta}\Delta^{\zeta}\mathbf{1}_{n+m=2,3}+\Delta^{\zeta}\mathbf{1}_{n+m\geq 4}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+{\bf 1}_{n\geq 2}\left(\Delta^{1/2}+\Delta^{1/2-\zeta}\right)d^{*}_{\epsilon,s}(n-1,m,k)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\mathbf{1}_{n\geq 1}\Delta^{1/2}\sum_{q=1}^{K}a^{*}_{\epsilon,s}(n-1+q,m),

and
a^{*}_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,1)
is estimated as
\epsilon\Delta^{-1/2}
, for
n+m=2,3
and
\epsilon^{1+\zeta}\Delta^{-1+\zeta}
, for
n+m\geq 4
followed by the previous case since
\Delta\leq\tilde{\Delta}
. Note that (
5.2
) and (
5.2
) differ only on the initial condition because the first starts at time
s
while the second at time
k\Delta
.
Similarly, starting from later time than
s
, by (
5.15
), we obtain

\displaystyle d^{*}_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,k)\lesssim\frac{\epsilon^{2-2\zeta}}{\Delta^{1/2}}+\mathbf{1}_{n+m\geq 2}\epsilon^{1+\zeta}\Delta^{\zeta},\qquad k=2,\dots,l+1

while
d^{*}_{\epsilon,s}(n,m,1)
is bounded by
\epsilon^{1+\zeta}\Delta^{-1+\zeta}
,
\epsilon^{1+\zeta}\Delta^{-1/2}
and
\epsilon^{1+\zeta}\Delta^{-1+\zeta}
for
n=m=1
and
n\geq 2,m\geq 1
, and
n=1,m\geq 2
respectively. Similarly to the proof of Theorem
2.19
in Section
4.1
, we write (
4.62
) with
N^{*}
given in (
2.33
), i.e,

\underline{x}(k;m)\leq A_{\epsilon,\Delta}\,\underline{x}(k-1;m)+\Lambda_{\epsilon,\Delta}(k;m)\underline{x}(k;m)+u_{\epsilon,\Delta}(k;m),\;\;\;\;k=1,\dots,l+1

(5.22)

where

\displaystyle\underline{x}(k;m)

\displaystyle=[a^{*}_{\epsilon}(1,m,k)\,\cdots a^{*}_{\epsilon}(N^{*},m,k)\,\,d^{*}_{\epsilon}(1,m,k)\,\cdots\,d^{*}_{\epsilon}(N^{*},m,k)]^{T}

and

\displaystyle u_{\epsilon,\Delta}(k;m)=[u_{\epsilon,\Delta}(1,m,k)\,\cdots\,u_{\epsilon,\Delta}(2N^{*},m,k)]^{T}

for
k=1,\dots,l+1
.
We choose
\Delta<\tilde{\Delta}
such that
\|\Lambda_{\epsilon,\Delta}(k;m)\|_{\infty}\leq 1/2
(or any other constant less than 1). For every
k=1,\dots,l+1
, the matrices
\Lambda_{\epsilon,\Delta}(k;m)
and
b_{\epsilon,\Delta}(k;m)

are uniformly bounded by
1/2
and some constant
c
, respectively, independently of
\epsilon
.
Consequently, all components of
\underline{x}(k;m)
are uniformly bounded in
\epsilon
for all
k=1,\dots,l+1
,
which proves the result.

6
Tightness

In order to prove tightness,
we first observe that the space
\mdmathbb{S}
is a nuclear Fréchet space when endowed with the seminorms defined in (
2.17
). As a consequence, in order to prove tightness, we can use Mitoma’s criterium (that we recall below), so that it is enough to show tightness of the sequence of real-valued processes
\{Y^{\epsilon}_{t}(H)\}_{\epsilon>0}
, for every
H\in\mdmathbb{S}
.

Theorem 6.1

(Mitoma’s criterium - Theorem 4.1 of
[
19
]
)
.

A sequence of processes
\{X^{\epsilon}_{t};t\in[0,T]\}_{\epsilon>0}
in
\mathcal{D}([0,T],\mdmathbb{S}^{\prime})
is tight with respect to the Skorohod topology if, and only if, for every
H\in\mdmathbb{S}
, the sequence of real-valued processes
\{X^{\epsilon}_{t}(H);t\in[0,T]\}_{\epsilon}
is tight with respect to the Skorohod topology of
\mathcal{D}([0,T],\mdmathbb{R})
.

Recall (
3.3
). In order to show that
\{Y^{\epsilon}_{t}(H)\}_{\epsilon>0}
is tight, it is enough to show that

\{Y^{\epsilon}_{0}(H)\}_{\epsilon>0}\ ,\ \{[M^{\epsilon}_{t}(H)]_{t\geq 0}\}_{\epsilon>0}\textrm{ and }\left\{\int_{0}^{t}(\partial_{s}+L_{\epsilon})Y^{\epsilon}_{s}(H)ds\right\}_{\epsilon>0}

are tight.

6.1
Initial time

In this subsection we show that
\{Y^{\epsilon}_{0}(H)\}_{\epsilon>0}
is tight.
To this end, it is enough to show that

\lim_{A\to\infty}\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\mdmathbb{P}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[|Y_{0}^{\epsilon}(H)|>A]=0.

From (
2.19
) and from
Markov’s inequality, for every
A>0
and for every
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\mdmathbb{P}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[|Y_{0}^{\epsilon}(H)|>A]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{A^{2}}\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[|Y_{0}^{\epsilon}(H)|^{2}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\epsilon}{A^{2}}\Big(\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}[H_{0}(\epsilon x)]^{2}\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[(\eta_{0}(x)-\rho^{\epsilon}_{0}(x))^{2}]+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in\Lambda_{N}\\
y\neq x\end{subarray}}H_{0}(\epsilon x)H_{0}(\epsilon y)C^{2,\epsilon}_{0}(x,y),

where
C_{t}^{2,\epsilon}(x,y)
was defined in (
2.20
). Note that
C^{2,\epsilon}_{0}(x,y)\equiv 0
given the choice of our initial measure, see
Assumption 1
. Therefore, the rightmost term in last display is equal to zero, and
the leftmost term in last display is of order
O(A^{-2})
and so the limit is proved.
In fact, we observe that we can prove the convergence of the fluctuation field at time
t=0
to a Gaussian process by using characteristic functions, in the following manner:

\begin{split}\log\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[\exp\{iY_{0}^{\epsilon}(H)\}]&=\log\prod_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[\exp\{i\sqrt{\epsilon}H(\epsilon x)(\eta_{0}(x)-\rho^{\epsilon}_{0}(x))\}]\\
&=\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}\log\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[\exp\{i\sqrt{\epsilon}H(\epsilon x)(\eta_{0}(x)-\rho^{\epsilon}_{0}(x))\}]\end{split}

(6.1)

and by Taylor expansion the last display can be written as :

\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}\log\Big(1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}H^{2}(\epsilon x)E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[(\eta_{0}(x)-\rho^{\epsilon}_{0}(x))^{2}]+O(\epsilon^{3/2})\Big),

(6.2)

but again from Taylor expansion, from
Assumption 1
and since
u_{0}
is a smooth function the last display converges, as
\epsilon\to 0
, to
-\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}H^{2}(u)u_{0}(u)du.
This shows that the characteristic function of the initial density field
Y_{0}(H)
coincides with the one of the Gaussian process with the variance given above.

6.2
The integral terms

To prove tightness of the integral terms we use the Kolmogorov-Centsov’s criterion, that we state here for convenience.

Proposition 6.2

(Kolmogorov-Centsov criterion - Problem 2.4.11 of
[
13
]
)
.

A sequence
\{X^{\epsilon}_{t};t\in[0,T]\}_{\epsilon>0}
of
continuous, real-valued, stochastic processes is tight with respect to the uniform topology of
\mathcal{C}([0,T];\mdmathbb{R})
if the sequence of real-valued random variables
\{X^{\epsilon}_{0}\}_{\epsilon>0}
is tight and there are constants
K,{}_{1},{}_{2}>0
such that, for any
t,s\in[0,T]
and any
\epsilon>0
, it holds that

\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[|X^{\epsilon}_{t}-X^{\epsilon}_{s}|^{{}_{1}}]\leq K|t-s|^{1+{}_{2}}.

6.2.1
The bulk term

We first treat the bulk term, i.e., the term of the form

\mathbf{Z}_{t}^{\epsilon}(H):=\int_{0}^{t}Y_{s}^{\epsilon}(\Delta_{\epsilon}H)ds.

We note that from (
2.19
) for any
H\in\mdmathbb S
, we have that

Y^{\epsilon}_{s}(H)\;=\;\sqrt{\epsilon}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}H_{s}(\epsilon x)\Big(\eta_{s}(x)-\rho_{\epsilon}(x,ss)\Big)+\sqrt{\epsilon}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}H_{s}(\epsilon x)\Big(\rho_{\epsilon}(x,s)-\rho^{\epsilon}_{s}(x)\Big),

where
\rho_{\epsilon}(s,x)
is the solution to (
2.10
).

From (
2.29
), the rightmost term in last display equals to

-\sqrt{\epsilon}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}H(s,\epsilon x)v_{{1}}^{\epsilon}(x,s|\mu^{\epsilon})

and now we treat separately the two cases.
First note that if

\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\epsilon}=\int_{0}^{t}ds\,\sqrt{\epsilon}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}\Delta_{\epsilon}H_{s}(\epsilon x)\Big(\eta_{s}(x)-\rho_{\epsilon}(x,s)\Big),

(6.3)

then by squaring the integral we have that

\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\left[|\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\epsilon}-\mathbf{X}_{s}^{\epsilon}|^{2}\right]=\epsilon\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r}dr\,d\tau\sum_{x,y\in\Lambda_{N}}\Delta_{\epsilon}H_{r}(\epsilon x)\Delta_{\epsilon}H_{\tau}(\epsilon y){v^{\epsilon,1,1}_{\tau,y}(r,x)}.

(6.4)

Since
H\in\mdmathbb S
and by using Theorem
2.24
, the last display is bounded from above by:

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-1}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r}dr\,d\tau\sup_{x,y}|v^{\epsilon,1,1}_{\tau,r}(x,y)|

(6.5)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-1}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r}dr\,d\tau\sup_{x,y}|v^{\epsilon,1,1}_{\tau,r}(x,y)|\left({\bf 1}_{r-\tau\leq\tilde{\Delta}}+{\bf 1}_{r-\tau>\tilde{\Delta}}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left((t-s)^{3/2}+(t-s)^{2}\right).

(6.6)

Now, for

\mathbf{Y}^{\epsilon}_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}ds\sqrt{\epsilon}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}\Delta_{\epsilon}H_{s}(\epsilon x)v_{n}^{\epsilon}(x,s|\mu^{\epsilon})

we evaluate as

\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\left[|\mathbf{Y}_{t}^{\epsilon}-\mathbf{Y}_{s}^{\epsilon}|^{2}\right]=\epsilon\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r}dr\,d\tau\sum_{x,y\in\Lambda_{N}}\Delta_{\epsilon}H_{r}(\epsilon x)\Delta_{\epsilon}H_{\tau},(\epsilon y)v_{n}^{\epsilon}(x,r|\mu^{\epsilon})v_{n}^{\epsilon}(y,\tau|\mu^{\epsilon})

(6.7)

and as above, by using Theorem
2.19
, this last display is bounded from above by

\epsilon^{-1}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r}dr\,d\tau\sup_{x,y}v_{1}^{\epsilon}(x,r|\mu^{\epsilon})v_{1}^{\epsilon}(y,\tau|\mu^{\epsilon})\lesssim\epsilon^{1-4\zeta}(t-s)^{2}.

(6.8)

6.2.2
The boundary terms

For the boundary terms we define

\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\epsilon}=\int_{0}^{t}ds\epsilon^{-1/2}\left(\bar{\eta}_{s}(x)-\bar{\eta}_{s}(y)\right)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\epsilon}=\int_{0}^{t}ds\epsilon^{-1/2}\prod_{x\in\mathfrak{X}}\bar{\eta}_{s}(x)

with
\mathfrak{X}\subset I_{\pm}
.
Similar to what we did to treat the bulk term and (
6.5
)

\displaystyle\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\left[|\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\epsilon}-\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}_{s}^{\epsilon}|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-1}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r}dr\,d\tau\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\left[\left(\bar{\eta}_{r}(x)-\bar{\eta}_{r}(y)\right)\left(\bar{\eta}_{\tau}(x)-\bar{\eta}_{\tau}(y)\right)\right]

(6.9)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-1}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r}dr\,d\tau\sup_{w\in\{x,y\}}\left(|v^{\epsilon,1,1}_{\tau,r}(y,w)|+|v^{\epsilon,1,1}_{\tau,r}(x,w)|\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left((t-s)^{3/2}+(t-s)^{2}\right)

as well as
\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\left[|\hat{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\epsilon}-\hat{\mathbf{X}}_{s}^{\epsilon}|^{2}\right]
is similarly estimated. This concludes the proof of tightness for all the integral terms.
In the next subsection, we establish the tightness of the sequence of martingales. More precisely, we show that the sequence converges to a mean-zero Gaussian process, which in particular implies its tightness.

6.3
Convergence of the sequence of martingales

In this section we prove the convergence of the sequence of martingales
\left\{M_{t}^{\epsilon}(H);t\in[0,T]\right\}_{\epsilon}
using Theorem VIII.3.12 in
[
12
]
. In fact, we use the statement of that theorem as in Proposition 4 of
[
1
]
:

Proposition 6.3

(
[
1
]
)
.

Let
t\in[0,T]\mapsto C_{t}\in[0,\infty)
be a deterministic continuous function of the time
t
. Let
\{M_{t}^{\epsilon};t\in[0,T]\}_{\epsilon}
be a sequence of square-integrable real-valued martingales with càdlàg trajectories defined on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mdmathbb P)
. Let
\{\langle M^{\epsilon}\rangle_{t};t\in[0,T]\}
denote the quadratic variation of
\{M_{t}^{\epsilon}\;;\;t\in[0,T]\}
. Assume that

i)

For each
\epsilon>0
, the quadratic variation process
\{\langle M^{\epsilon}\rangle_{t};t\in[0,T]\}
has continuous trajectories
\mdmathbb P
a.s.;

ii)

the maximal jump satisfies

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mdmathbb E\Big[\sup_{0\leq s\leq T}\big|M_{s}^{\epsilon}-M_{s-}^{\epsilon}\big|\Big]=0;

(6.10)

Above
\mdmathbb E
denotes the expectation w.r.t.
\mdmathbb P
.

iii)

For each
t\in[0,T]
, the sequence of random variables
\{\langle M^{\epsilon}\rangle_{t}\}_{\epsilon}
converges in probability to the deterministic path
\{C_{t}\;;\;t\in[0,T]\}
.

Then the sequence
\{M_{t}^{\epsilon};t\in[0,T]\}_{\epsilon}
converges in law in
\mathcal{D}([0,T],\mdmathbb R)
to a martingale
\{M_{t};t\in[0,T]\}
with quadratic variation
t\mapsto C_{t}
. Moreover
\{M_{t};t\in[0,T]\}
is a mean zero Gaussian process.

From the previous result we conclude that:

Corollary 6.4
.

For
H\in\mdmathbb S
, the sequence of martingales
\{M_{t}^{\epsilon}(H);t\in[0,T]\}_{\epsilon}
converges in the topology of
\mathcal{D}([0,T],\mdmathbb R)
, as
\epsilon\to 0
, towards a mean-zero Gaussian process
W_{t}(H)
with quadratic variation given by

\int_{0}^{t}\|\ T_{t-s}H\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{s})}^{2}\,ds,

where
\rho(u,s)
is the solution of the hydrodynamic equation (
2.5
) and the
\mdmathbb L^{2}
-norm is defined in (
2.26
).

Proof.

The last lemma is a simple consequence of
[
12
, Theorem VIII.3.12]
. First, we observe that ii) is a consequence of the fact that

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\Big[\sup_{s\leq t}\big|M^{\epsilon}_{s}(H)-M^{\epsilon}_{s^{-}}(H)\big|\Big]\;=\;0,

since at each time of a ring of a clock of a Poisson process, the configuration
\eta
only changes its value in at most two sites, which in turn implies that

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\sup_{s\leq t}\big|M_{s}^{\epsilon}(H)-M^{\epsilon}_{s^{-}}(H)\big|=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\sup_{s\leq t}\big|Y_{s}^{\epsilon}(H)-Y^{\epsilon}_{s^{-}}(H)\big|\leq\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\sqrt{\epsilon}2\|H\|_{\infty}=0.

For items i) and iii), we note that from (
3.4
), the quadratic variation of the martingale
M_{s}^{\epsilon}(H)
is given by

\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma^{\epsilon}_{s}(H_{s})ds
and
a simple computation shows that

\begin{split}\Gamma_{s}^{\epsilon}(H_{s})=\frac{\epsilon}{2}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}(\nabla^{+}_{\epsilon}H_{s}(\epsilon x))^{2}(\eta_{s}(x)-\eta_{s}(x+1))^{2}+\frac{j}{2}\sum_{x\in I_{\pm}}D_{\pm}\eta_{s}(x)(H_{s}(\epsilon x))^{2}.\end{split}

Item i) follows from the fact that the number of particles is bounded and from the observation that the integral in time of a bounded function is a continuous function
\mdmathbb P
a.s.

To analyse the convergence, we observe that,

\sum_{x\in I_{+}}D_{+}\eta(x)=1{-}\prod_{x\in I_{+}}\eta(x)

(6.11)

and

\sum_{x\in I_{-}}D_{-}\eta(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{K}(-1)^{j+1}\sum_{A_{j}\subset I_{-}}\prod_{x\in A_{j}}\eta(x),

(6.12)

where for
j=1,\dots,K
the sets
A_{j}\subset I_{-}
with cardinality
j
. Since the measure
\mu^{\epsilon}
satisfies (
2.8
), then, from the Replacement Lemma proved in
[
7
, Lemma A.3]
and by the hydrodynamic limit proved in
[
7
, Theorem 2.7]
we conclude that

\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma_{s}^{\epsilon}(H_{s})ds,

converges in distribution, as
\epsilon\to 0
, to

\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{t}\int_{-1}^{1}\chi(\rho_{s}(u))\big(\partial_{u}T_{t-s}H(u)\big)^{2}du\;ds\\
+&\int_{0}^{t}\frac{j}{2}(1-\rho_{s}(1)^{K})(H_{s}(1))^{2}+\frac{j}{2}\Big[1-(1-\rho_{s}(-1))^{K}\Big]H_{s}(-1))^{2}\,ds,\end{split}

and since last expression is deterministic, the convergence holds in probability.

∎

Appendix A
Appendix

In this appendix we collect several computations that have been used throughout the article. We begin by presenting explicit calculations concerning the action of the generator on the occupation variables.

A.1
The action of the generator

A simple computation shows that for
x=-(N-1),\dots,N-1

L_{0}\eta(x)=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\eta(x+1)+\eta(x-1)-2\eta(x)\Big)

and

L_{0}\eta(N)=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\eta(N-1)-\eta(N)\Big)
and
L_{0}\eta(-N)=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\eta(-(N-1))-\eta(-N)\Big)
. To be consistent with the notation introduced above, see (
2.12
) we write
L_{0}\eta(x)=\frac{1}{2}\Delta\eta(x).

Now, for the boundary action, we note that a simple computation shows that

L_{b,\pm}\eta(x)=\frac{j}{2}D_{\pm}\eta(x)(1-2\eta(x))

for
x\in I_{\pm}
.

The discrete profile:

Recall (
2.9
).
From the previous computations and Kolmogorov’s equation we can conclude that

\partial_{t}\rho_{t}^{\epsilon}(x)=\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{\epsilon}\rho_{t}^{\epsilon}(x)+\frac{\epsilon^{-1}j}{2}\textbf{1}_{x\in I_{+}}\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[D_{+}\eta_{s}(x)]-\frac{\epsilon^{-1}j}{2}\textbf{1}_{x\in I_{-}}\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[D_{-}\eta_{s}(x)].

(A.1)

The action on the density field:

Now we compute the action of the generator on the density fluctuation field. A simple computation shows that

L_{0}Y^{\epsilon}_{s}(H)=\sqrt{\epsilon}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}H(\epsilon x)\Delta\eta_{s}(x)=\sqrt{\epsilon}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}\Delta H(\epsilon x)\eta_{s}(x),

L_{b}Y^{\epsilon}_{s}(H)=\sqrt{\epsilon}\frac{j}{2}\sum_{x\in I_{\pm}}H(\epsilon x)D_{\pm}\eta_{s}(x).

Recall (
2.1
), then

L_{\epsilon}Y^{\epsilon}_{s}(H)=\sqrt{\epsilon}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}\Delta_{\epsilon}H(\epsilon x)\eta_{s}(x)+\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}\sum_{x\in I_{\pm}}H(\epsilon x)D_{\pm}\eta_{s}(x).

Moreover, from (
A.1
) we see that

\begin{split}\partial_{s}Y^{\epsilon}_{s}(H)&=-\sqrt{\epsilon}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}H(\epsilon x)\Delta_{\epsilon}\rho_{t}^{\epsilon}(x)\\
&-\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}\sum_{x\in I_{+}}H(\epsilon x)\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[D_{+}\eta_{s}(x)]+\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}\sum_{x\in I_{-}}H(\epsilon x)\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[D_{-}\eta_{s}(x)].\end{split}

By a summation by parts last expression becomes

\begin{split}\partial_{s}Y^{\epsilon}_{s}(H)&=\sqrt{\epsilon}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}\Delta_{\epsilon}H(\epsilon x)\rho_{s}^{\epsilon}(x)\\
&+\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}\sum_{x\in I_{+}}H(\epsilon x)\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[D_{+}\eta_{s}(x)]-\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}\sum_{x\in I_{-}}H(\epsilon x)\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[D_{-}\eta_{s}(x)].\end{split}

A.2
Treating boundary terms

We recall that just below (
3.7
), in order to give a more transparent presentation of the closure of the martingale, we presented it for the case
K=2
. Below, for completeness, we repeat the computations for a general
K
. To that end, recall the form of
D_{+}
given in (
2.4
). We have the identity

\sum_{x\in I_{+}}\Big(D_{+}\eta(x)-\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[D_{+}\eta(x)]\Big)=-\Big(\prod_{x\in I_{+}}\eta(x)-\mdmathbb{E}^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\Big[\prod_{x\in I_{+}}\eta(x)\Big]\Big).

(A.2)

Denoting for
j=1,\dots,K
by
A_{j}
the subsets of
I_{+}
with cardinality equal to
j
, we observe that

\begin{split}\prod_{x\in I_{+}}\eta(x)&=\sum_{j=1}^{K}\sum_{A_{j}\subset I_{+}}\Big\{\prod_{x\in A_{j}}\bar{\eta}(x)\prod_{y\in A_{j}^{c}}\rho_{\epsilon}(y)\Big\}+\prod_{x\in I_{+}}\rho_{\epsilon}(x)\\
&=\sum_{x\in I_{+}}\Big\{\bar{\eta}(x)\prod_{y\in I_{+}\setminus\{x\}}\rho_{\epsilon}(y)\Big\}+\sum_{j=2}^{K}\sum_{A_{j}\subset I_{+}}\Big\{\prod_{x\in A_{j}}\bar{\eta}(x)\prod_{y\in A_{j}^{c}}\rho_{\epsilon}(y)\Big\}+\prod_{x\in I_{+}}\rho_{\epsilon}(x).\end{split}

Now we focus on the leftmost term on the last line of last display and we rewrite it as

\begin{split}&\sum_{x\in I_{+}\setminus\{N\}}\Big\{\bar{\eta}(x)\prod_{y\in I_{+}\setminus\{x\}}\rho_{\epsilon}(y)\Big\}+\bar{\eta}(N)\prod_{y\in I_{+}\setminus\{N\}}\rho_{\epsilon}(y)\\
=&\sum_{x\in I_{+}\setminus\{N\}}\Big\{(\bar{\eta}(x)-\bar{\eta}(N))\prod_{y\in I_{+}\setminus\{x\}}\rho_{\epsilon}(y)\Big\}+\bar{\eta}(N)\sum_{x\in I_{+}}\prod_{y\in I_{+}\setminus\{x\}}\rho_{\epsilon}(y).\end{split}

We write the rightmost term of last display as

\begin{split}\bar{\eta}(N)\sum_{x\in I_{+}}\prod_{y\in I_{+}\setminus\{x\}}(\rho_{\epsilon}(y)-\rho_{\epsilon}(N))+K\bar{\eta}(N)(\rho_{\epsilon}(N))^{K-1}.\end{split}

Putting together all the computations we get that

\begin{split}\prod_{x\in I_{+}}\eta(x)&=\sum_{j=2}^{K}\sum_{A_{j}\subset I_{+}}\Big\{\prod_{x\in A_{j}}\bar{\eta}(x)\prod_{y\in A_{j}^{c}}\rho_{\epsilon}(y)\Big\}+\prod_{x\in I_{+}}\rho_{\epsilon}(x)\\
&+\sum_{x\in I_{+}\setminus\{N\}}\Big\{(\bar{\eta}(x)-\bar{\eta}(N))\prod_{y\in I_{+}\setminus\{x\}}\rho_{\epsilon}(y)\Big\}\\
&+\bar{\eta}(N)\sum_{x\in I_{+}}\prod_{y\in I_{+}\setminus\{x\}}(\rho_{\epsilon}(y)-\rho_{\epsilon}(N))+K\bar{\eta}(N)(\rho_{\epsilon}(N))^{K-1}.\end{split}

From this we conclude that

\begin{split}\prod_{x\in I_{+}}\eta(x)&=\sum_{j=2}^{K}\sum_{A_{j}\subset I_{+}}\Big\{\prod_{x\in A_{j}}\bar{\eta}(x)\prod_{y\in A_{j}^{c}}\rho_{\epsilon}(y)\Big\}+\prod_{x\in I_{+}}\rho_{\epsilon}(x)\\
&+\sum_{x\in I_{+}\setminus\{N\}}\Big\{(\bar{\eta}(x)-\bar{\eta}(N))\prod_{y\in I_{+}\setminus\{x\}}\rho_{\epsilon}(y)\Big\}\\
&+\bar{\eta}(N)\sum_{x\in I_{+}}\prod_{y\in I_{+}\setminus\{x\}}(\rho_{\epsilon}(y)-\rho_{\epsilon}(N))+K\bar{\eta}(N)(\rho_{\epsilon}(N))^{K-1}.\end{split}

(A.3)

Observe that the expectation of the last term equals to

\begin{split}\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\Big[\prod_{x\in I_{+}}\eta(x)\Big]&=\sum_{j=2}^{K}\sum_{A_{j}\subset I_{+}}\Big\{\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\Big[\prod_{x\in A_{j}}\bar{\eta}(x)\Big]\prod_{y\in A_{j}^{c}}\rho_{\epsilon}(y)\Big\}+\prod_{x\in I_{+}}\rho_{\epsilon}(x)\\
&+\sum_{x\in I_{+}\setminus\{N\}}\Big\{(\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[\bar{\eta}(x)]-\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[\bar{\eta}(N)])\prod_{y\in I_{+}\setminus\{x\}}\rho_{\epsilon}(y)\Big\}\\
&+\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[\bar{\eta}(N)]\sum_{x\in I_{+}}\prod_{y\in I_{+}\setminus\{x\}}(\rho_{\epsilon}(y)-\rho_{\epsilon}(N))+K\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[\bar{\eta}(N)](\rho_{\epsilon}(N))^{K-1}.\end{split}

(A.4)

Putting together (
A.3
) and (
A.4
) we see that

\begin{split}&\sum_{x\in I_{+}}\Big(D_{+}\eta(x)-\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[D_{+}\eta(x)]\Big)\\
=-&\sum_{j=2}^{K}\sum_{A_{j}\subset I_{+}}\Big\{\prod_{x\in A_{j}}\bar{\eta}(x)\prod_{y\in A_{j}^{c}}\rho_{\epsilon}(y)\Big\}+\sum_{j=2}^{K}\sum_{A_{j}\subset I_{+}}\Big\{\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\Big[\prod_{x\in A_{j}}\bar{\eta}(x)\Big]\prod_{y\in A_{j}^{c}}\rho_{\epsilon}(y)\Big\}\\
-&\sum_{x\in I_{+}\setminus\{N\}}\Big\{(\bar{\eta}(x)-\bar{\eta}(N))\prod_{y\in I_{+}\setminus\{x\}}\rho_{\epsilon}(y)\Big\}+\sum_{x\in I_{+}\setminus\{N\}}\Big\{(\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[\bar{\eta}(x)]-\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[\bar{\eta}(N)])\prod_{y\in I_{+}\setminus\{x\}}\rho_{\epsilon}(y)\Big\}\\
-&\bar{\eta}(N)\sum_{x\in I_{+}}\prod_{y\in I_{+}\setminus\{x\}}(\rho_{\epsilon}(y)-\rho_{\epsilon}(N))-K\bar{\eta}(N)(\rho_{\epsilon}(N))^{K-1}\\
+&\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[\bar{\eta}(N)]\sum_{x\in I_{+}}\prod_{y\in I_{+}\setminus\{x\}}(\rho_{\epsilon}(y)-\rho_{\epsilon}(N))+K\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[\bar{\eta}(N)](\rho_{\epsilon}(N))^{K-1}.\end{split}

(A.5)

For the left boundary the computations are completely analogous and for that reason they are omitted.
Now we are back to (
3.7
).
From the computations presented above (and now we added the time dependence), the leftmost term in (
3.7
) can be rewritten as

\begin{split}&\underbrace{-\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)\sum_{j=2}^{K}\sum_{A_{j}\subset I_{+}}\Big\{\prod_{x\in A_{j}}\bar{\eta}_{s}(x)\prod_{y\in A_{j}^{c}}\rho_{\epsilon}(y,s)\Big\}}_{\text{A}}\\
+&\underbrace{\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)\sum_{j=2}^{K}\sum_{A_{j}\subset I_{+}}\Big\{v_{|A_{j}|}^{\epsilon}(A_{j},s)\prod_{y\in A_{j}^{c}}\rho_{\epsilon}(y,s)\Big\}}_{\text{B}}\\
-&\underbrace{\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)\sum_{x\in I_{+}\setminus\{N\}}\Big\{(\bar{\eta}_{s}(x)-\bar{\eta}_{s}(N))\prod_{y\in I_{+}\setminus\{x\}}\rho_{\epsilon}(y,s)\Big\}}_{\text{C}}\\
+&\underbrace{{\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)\sum_{x\in I_{+}\setminus\{N\}}\Big\{(\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[\bar{\eta}_{s}(x)]-\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)])\prod_{y\in I_{+}\setminus\{x\}}\rho_{\epsilon}(y,s)\Big\}}}_{\text{D}}\\
-&\underbrace{\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)\sum_{x\in I_{+}}\prod_{y\in I_{+}\setminus\{x\}}(\rho_{\epsilon}(y,s)-\rho_{\epsilon}(N,s))}_{\text{E}}\\
-&\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)K\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)(\rho_{\epsilon}(N,s))^{K-1}.\end{split}

We can obtain analogous identities with respect to the left boundary but we omit them.
Observe that the
\mdmathbb L^{2}
-norm of the time integral of the term A can be bounded from above by a constant

C_{j,\psi,K}\epsilon^{\zeta}(t^{1+\zeta}\mathbf{1}_{t<1}+t^{2}\mathbf{1}_{t\geq 1})
for some
\zeta>0
, using Theorem
2.24
. Indeed, since
|\rho_{\epsilon}(s,y)|\leq 1
for all
s
and
y
, we have that

\begin{split}&\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\Big[\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)\sum_{j=2}^{K}\sum_{A_{j}\subset I_{+}}\Big\{\prod_{x\in A_{j}}\bar{\eta}_{s}(x)\prod_{y\in A_{j}^{c}}\rho_{\epsilon}(y,s)\Big\}\Big)^{2}\Big]\\
\leq&\sum_{j=2}^{K}\sum_{A_{j}\subset I_{+}}\frac{C_{j,\psi}}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{r}\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}\Big[\prod_{x\in A_{j}}\bar{\eta}_{s}(x)\prod_{\tilde{x}\in A_{j}}\bar{\eta}_{r}(\tilde{x})\Big]dsdr\leq C_{j,\psi,K}\epsilon^{\zeta}(t^{1+\zeta}\mathbf{1}_{t<1}+t^{2}\mathbf{1}_{t\geq 1}).\end{split}

Analogously, from Theorem
2.24
the
\mdmathbb L^{2}
-norm of the time integral of the term B can be bounded from above by
\epsilon
to some positive exponent. From Corollary
2.25
, the
\mdmathbb L^{2}
-norm of the time integral of the term C can be bounded from above by
C_{j,\psi,K}\epsilon^{\zeta}(t^{1+\zeta}\mathbf{1}_{t<1}+t^{2}\mathbf{1}_{t\geq 1})
for some
\zeta>0
. Since
\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[\bar{\eta}_{s}(x)]=v^{\epsilon}_{1}(x,s)
and
\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)]=v^{\epsilon}_{1}(N,s)
, by Theorem
2.19
, the time integral of the term D is bounded as follows

\int_{0}^{t}\frac{j}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{}_{s}(1)\sum_{x\in I_{+}\setminus\{N\}}\Big\{(\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[\bar{\eta}_{s}(x)]-\mdmathbb E^{\epsilon}_{\mu^{\epsilon}}[\bar{\eta}_{s}(N)])\prod_{y\in I_{+}\setminus\{x\}}\rho_{\epsilon}(y,s)\Big\}ds\leq C_{j,\psi,K}\frac{\epsilon^{1-2\zeta}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}t,

where we use
\prod_{y\in I_{+}\setminus\{x\}}|\rho_{\epsilon}(s,y)|\leq 1
. Finally,
to compute the
\mdmathbb L^{2}
-norm of the time integral of the term E we use Corollary
D.2
. We leave the details to the reader but it is enough to follow the same steps as presented above for the case
K=2
. Now, from this, the rightmost term in the second line and the third line of (
3.6
) is equal to

\begin{split}-&\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}\Big(\partial_{u}{}_{s}(1){+}jK(\rho_{s}(1))^{K-1}{}_{s}(1)\Big)\bar{\eta}_{s}(N),\end{split}

(A.6)

from where we see that (
2.18
) implies that the last display is equal to zero. This finishes the closure of the martingale for any value of
K
.

Appendix B
A single random walk with reflections

In this section we state some properties of a single random walk in
\Lambda_{N}
with reflections
at
\pm N
, referring to
[
4
]
for the proofs.

Let
Q_{t}^{(\epsilon)}(x,y)
denote the transition probability of a
simple random walk in
\mdmathbb{Z}
with jumps of intensity

\epsilon^{-2}/2
between nearest neighbor (
n.n.
) sites, and let

P_{t}^{(\epsilon)}(x,y)
denote the transition probability of a
corresponding random walk on
\Lambda_{N}
, with the jumps outside
\Lambda_{N}

being suppressed (and the same jump rates within
\Lambda_{N}
).

P_{t}^{(\epsilon)}
and
Q_{t}^{(\epsilon)}
are thus related via the
“reflection map”
{}_{N}:\mdmathbb Z\to\Lambda_{N}
defined as follows.

•

|x|\leq N
:
{}_{N}(x)=x
.

•

x<-N
:
{}_{N}(x)=-{}_{N}(-x)
;

•

x>N
:
{}_{N}\left(N+j(2N+1)+k\right)={}_{N}\left(N+j(2N+1)-(k-1)\right)
,

k=1,\dots,2N+1,j=0,1,\dots
.

From the last identification we see that the two transition probabilities are related as follows

P^{(\epsilon)}_{t}(x,z)=\sum_{y:{}_{N}(y)=z}Q^{(\epsilon)}_{t}(x,y).

(B.1)

Let

G_{t}(x,y)=\frac{e^{-(x-y)^{2}/2t}}{\sqrt{2\pi t}}.

(B.2)

By the local central limit theorem,
[
16
]
,
there exist positive constants
c_{1},...,c_{5}
so that for
|x-y|\leq(\epsilon^{-2}t)^{5/8}
it holds:

|Q^{(\epsilon)}_{t}(x,y)-G_{\epsilon^{-2}t}(x,y)|\leq\frac{c_{1}}{\sqrt{\epsilon^{-2}t}}G_{\epsilon^{-2}t}(x,y),\quad

(B.3)

while for
|x-y|>(\epsilon^{-2}t)^{5/8}
it holds:

Q^{(\epsilon)}_{t}(x,y)\leq\min\Big\{c_{2}e^{-c_{3}|x-y|^{2}/(\epsilon^{-2}t)},c_{4}e^{-|y-x|(\log|y-x|-c_{5})}\Big\}.

(B.4)

As a corollary, for any
T>0
there exists
c
so that the following holds:

•

For all
\epsilon
, all
t\in(0,T]
and all
x
,
y
in
\Lambda_{N}

P^{(\epsilon)}_{t}(x,y)\leq c\;G_{\epsilon^{-2}t}(x,y).

(B.5)

Remark B.1
.

We observe that in fact, due to non-integrability of the heat kernel close to zero, we used along the paper the estimate

P^{(\epsilon)}_{t}(x,y)\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{\epsilon^{-2}t}+1}.

(B.6)

One way to prove this estimate is to do the following. To simplify notation we denote
\lambda:=\epsilon^{-2}t
.
First we note that since we are dealing with a probability we have that

P^{(\epsilon)}_{t}(x,y)\leq\min\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}},1\}.

(B.7)

To show the bound we do the following. First we split into two cases:

–

\sqrt{\lambda}>1
, in this case the minimum is attained at
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}
. Now we note that
since
\sqrt{\lambda}>1
then
2\sqrt{\lambda}>\sqrt{\lambda}+1
from where we get that
\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\lambda}}<\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}+1}
. From this we get that

P^{(\epsilon)}_{t}(x,y)\leq\min\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}},1\}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}<\frac{2}{\sqrt{\lambda}+1}.

(B.8)

–

\sqrt{\lambda}<1
, in this case the minimum is attained at
1
. Now we note that
since
\sqrt{\lambda}<1
then
2>\sqrt{\lambda}+1
from where we get that
\frac{1}{2}<\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}+1}
. From this we get that

P^{(\epsilon)}_{t}(x,y)\leq\min\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}},1\}=1<\frac{2}{\sqrt{\lambda}+1}.

(B.9)

This observation also extends to the heat kernel.

•

For all
\epsilon
, all
t\in(0,T]
and all

-N\leq x\leq N-1
,

\Big|P^{(\epsilon)}_{t}(x,y)-P^{(\epsilon)}_{t}(x+1,y)\Big|\leq\frac{c}{\sqrt{\epsilon^{-2}t}}G_{\epsilon^{-2}t}(x,y).

(B.10)

Note that

G_{\epsilon^{-2}t}(x,y)\leq c\frac{1}{[\epsilon^{-2}t]^{1/2}+1}.

(B.11)

As above we can also replace the last estimate by

\Big|P^{(\epsilon)}_{t}(x,y)-P^{(\epsilon)}_{t}(x+1,y)\Big|\leq\frac{c}{\sqrt{\epsilon^{-2}t}+1}G_{\epsilon^{-2}t}(x,y).

(B.12)

Appendix C
Background on the stirring process

Before we proceed with the proofs, we start with the following definitions:

•

Active/Passive marks process

•

Labeled process

•

Stopping time

Definition C.1

( The active/passive marks process)
.

\bullet
The active/passive marks process is realized in a probability space denoted by
(\Omega,\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon})
.
It is a product of Poisson processes indexed by
\{x,x+1\}
,
x\in\mdmathbb Z
: for each pair
\{x,x+1\}

we have a Poisson point process of intensity
\epsilon^{-2}
, its events are called “marks” and each mark
is independently given the attribute “active” or “passive” with probability
1/2
. The processes
relative to different pairs are mutually independent and their common law is
\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}
, its expectation
being also denoted by
\mathbf{E}_{\epsilon}
.

\bullet
For any
\omega\in\Omega
we define the following particle
evolution in
\Lambda_{N}
: a particle at
x\in\Lambda_{N}
moves as soon as an
active mark appears at a pair
x,y
with
y\in\Lambda_{N}
. The particle
then moves to
y
(note that if another particle was at
y
then it
would simultaneously move to
x
). Passive marks do not play any
role so far as well as all marks
\{x,x+1\}
with either
x
or

x+1
not in
\Lambda_{N}
; they will be used later to construct
couplings. We denote by
X(t)\subset\Lambda_{N}
the set of all sites
occupied by the particles at time
t
, so that
\eta(x,t)=1
iff

x\in X(t)
.

Definition C.2

(The labeled process)
.

Given
\omega\in\Omega
, we can follow unambiguously the motion of each
individual particle so that we can give them labels at time 0
which then remain attached to the particles during their motion. We
shall denote by
\underline{x}=(x_{i_{1}},\dots,x_{i_{n}})
a labeled
configuration of
n
particles, (
i_{1},\dots,i_{n}
the labels,

x_{i_{j}}
the positions); configurations obtained under permutations
of the labels are now considered distinct. We write
\underline{x}(t)
for
the labeled process induced by the active/passive marks and denote
by
X(t)
the unlabeled configuration obtained from
\underline{x}(t)
,
(i.e. only the positions in
\underline{x}(t)
are recorded by
X(t)
). By
an abuse of notation we shall write
\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}
and
\mathbf{E}_{\epsilon}
both
for law and expectation in the active/passive marks process and for
the marginal over
\underline{x}(t)
, the labeled process realized in
this space. By adding a subscript
\underline{x}
we mean that the
initial distribution of particles has support on the single labeled
configuration
\underline{x}
.

Definition C.3

(The variables
\mathcal{T}_{x_{1},x_{2}}
,
\tau_{x_{1},x_{2}}
and
N_{x_{1},x_{2},t}
)
.

Given the initial position
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
of particles
1
and
2

define in
\Omega
the random multi-interval
\mathcal{T}_{x_{1},x_{2}}=\{s\geq 0:|x_{1}(s)-x_{2}(s)|=1\}
, calling
\mathcal{T}_{x_{1},x_{2},t}:=\mathcal{T}_{x_{1},x_{2}}\cap[0,t]
and
I_{x_{1},x_{2},t}=\{(s,y_{1},y_{2}):s\in\mathcal{T}_{x_{1},x_{2},t},y_{i}=x_{i}(s)
and a mark
appears at
s
between
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
\}
, we define the stopping
time
\tau_{x_{1},x_{2}}
as the smallest
s
in
I_{x_{1},x_{2},\infty}

and
N_{x_{1},x_{2},t}
the total number of elements in

I_{x_{1},x_{2},t}
. Notice that the presence of other particles does
not affect the values of
\mathcal{T}_{x_{1},x_{2}}
,
\tau_{x_{1},x_{2}}

and
N_{x_{1},x_{2},t}
.

Lemma C.4

(
[
6
]
, Lemma 4.3)
.

Let
\underline{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
,
t>s>0
and
f(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
a
function antisymmetric under the exchange of
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
. Then

\mathbf{E}_{\epsilon,\underline{x}}\Big[\mathbf{1}_{\tau_{x_{1},x_{2}}\leq s}f(\underline{x}(t))\Big]=0,

(C.1)

where the suffix
\underline{x}
indicates the initial condition i.e.

\mathbf{E}_{\epsilon,\underline{x}}(\cdot)=\mathbf{E}_{\epsilon}[\cdot|\underline{x}(0)=\underline{x}]
.

Definition C.5

(The stopping time
\tau_{i,j,t_{0}}
)
.

Let
\{\underline{x}(t)\}_{t\geq 0}
be the labeled process realized in the active/passive marks process , let
i
and
j
be the labels of two of its particles and
t_{0}\geq 0
.
We then define
\tau_{i,j,t_{0}}
as the first time
\tau>t_{0}

when (i)
|x_{i}(\tau)-x_{j}(\tau)|=1
and (ii) at
\tau
there is a mark (either active or passive) between
x_{i}(\tau)
and
x_{j}(\tau)
; otherwise we set
\tau=\infty
.
When
t_{0}=0
we just write
\tau_{i,j}
.

Theorem C.6
.

There is
c
so that for all
\epsilon>0

\sup_{|x_{1}-x_{2}|=1}\mdmathbb{P}_{\epsilon}\big[\tau_{x_{1},x_{2}}\geq t\big]\leq\frac{c}{(\epsilon^{-2}t)^{1/2}+1}.

(C.2)

Moreover, given any
T>0
, for any
\zeta>0
and
k\geq 1
there is

c
so that for all
t\leq T
and for all
\epsilon>0

\sup_{x_{1},x_{2}}\mdmathbb{P}_{\epsilon}\big[N_{x_{1},x_{2},t}\geq(\epsilon^{-2}t)^{1/2+\zeta}\big]\leq c(\epsilon^{-2}t)^{-k}.

(C.3)

C.1
Useful estimates for transition probabilities

Lemma C.7
.

Let
n\geq 2
. For any
\underline{z}=(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})
and
\underline{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
in
\Lambda_{N}^{n,\neq}
, the transition probability to go from
\underline{z}
to
\underline{y}
in a time
\lambda>0
is bounded as:

\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(\underline{y}\overset{\lambda}{\rightarrow}\underline{z})\leq c_{n}\frac{1}{{{[\epsilon^{-2}\lambda]}^{n/2}+1}}\leq c_{n}\frac{1}{{{[\epsilon^{-2}\lambda]}^{1-\zeta}}}

(C.4)

for some constant
c_{n}>0
which depends on
n
and for some small
\zeta>0
. For
n=1

\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(y\overset{\lambda}{\rightarrow}z)\leq c_{1}\frac{1}{{[\epsilon^{-2}\lambda]}^{1/2}+1}.

(C.5)

Proof.

By applying Liggett’s inequality
[
6
]
, we have that

\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(\underline{y}\overset{\lambda}{\rightarrow}\underline{z})\leq\prod_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}P^{(\epsilon)}_{\lambda}(y_{i},z_{j}).

(C.6)

Recall (
B.6
) we then get

\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(\underline{y}\overset{\lambda}{\rightarrow}\underline{z})\leq c_{n}\frac{1}{[\epsilon^{-2}\lambda]^{n/2}+1}

To finish the proof it is enough to note that
if
\epsilon^{-2}\lambda\geq 1
, since
n\geq 2
then
(\epsilon^{-2}\lambda)^{\frac{n}{2}}+1\geq(\epsilon^{-2}\lambda)^{\frac{n}{2}}\geq(\epsilon^{-2}\lambda)^{1-\zeta}
for some
\zeta>0
. From this the estimate follows. Now, if
\epsilon^{-2}\lambda\leq 1
, then

(\epsilon^{-2}\lambda)^{\frac{n}{2}}\leq(\epsilon^{-2}\lambda)^{1-\zeta}\leq 1\leq(\epsilon^{-2}\lambda)^{\frac{n}{2}}+1
and from this the proof ends.
∎

Remark C.8
.

Let
S_{n}
be the set of all permutations of the set
\{1,\dots,n\}
. We denote
\pi\in S_{n}
a permutation of
\{1,\dots,n\}
. Then, for
\underline{z}=(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})
and
\underline{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
in
\Lambda_{N}^{n,\neq}
, the right-hand side of (
C.6
), can be written as

\prod_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}P^{(\epsilon)}_{\lambda}(y_{i},z_{j})=\sum_{\pi\in S_{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}P^{(\epsilon)}_{\lambda}(y_{\pi(i)},z_{i})

which gives us

\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}(\underline{y}\overset{\lambda}{\rightarrow}\underline{z})\leq\sum_{\pi\in S_{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}P^{(\epsilon)}_{\lambda}(y_{\pi(i)},z_{i}).

(C.7)

C.2
Important bounds for space
v
-functions

For the difference of
v
’s, we use the next lemma:

Lemma C.9

(
[
6
]
, Lemma 5.3)
.

We have

\displaystyle|v(\underline{x}^{(i)},t)-v(\underline{x}^{(j)},t)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}ds\;\mathbf{E}_{\epsilon}\Big[\mathbf{1}_{\tau_{i,j}\geq\frac{s}{2}}\sum_{\underline{y}}\Big\{\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}\Big(\underline{x}^{(j)}\Big(\tfrac{s}{2}\Big)\overset{s/2}{\rightarrow}\underline{y}\Big)

(C.8)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{\epsilon}\Big(\underline{x}^{(i)}\Big(\tfrac{s}{2}\Big)\overset{s/2}{\rightarrow}\underline{y}\Big)\Big\}|(C_{\epsilon}v)(\underline{y},t-s)|\Big].

Now we provide the proof of Corollary
2.17
.

Proof.

When
\epsilon^{\beta^{*}}<t\leq T
, from Theorem
2.16
we have

\sup_{\epsilon^{\beta^{*}}<t\leq T}\sup_{\underline{x}\in\Lambda_{N}^{n,\neq}}|v^{\epsilon}_{n}(\underline{x},t|\mu^{\epsilon}|\leq c_{n}\epsilon^{(2-\beta^{*})c^{*}n}

for some
c_{n}
. For
\sup_{0<t\leq\epsilon^{\beta^{*}}}\sup_{\underline{x}\in\Lambda_{N}^{n,\neq}}|v^{\epsilon}_{n}(\underline{x},t)|
, we begin by rewriting (5.15)-(5.16) in
[
6
]
so that all
v^{\epsilon}_{\cdot}
appearing in the integral inequality are replaced by their suprema. Next, we take the supremum over

0<t\leq\epsilon^{\beta^{*}}
on both sides of (5.15) in
[
6
]
. On the right-hand side, this supremum transforms the time integral into one over the interval
(0,\epsilon^{\beta^{*}}]
. The resulting expression can then be estimated following the same argument used in the proof of Theorem 6.1 in
[
6
]
for
t=\epsilon^{\beta^{*}}
, ultimately yielding the desired bound.
∎

Appendix D
On the solutions to the discrete PDE (
2.10
)

Here we collected some results about the solution to (
2.10
). The first one was derived in
[
6
]
and the second one is a refinement of the former using a different method.

Proposition D.1

(
[
6
]
, eq. (3.8)–(3.9))
.

For any
T>0
there exists a finite constant
c
so that for any solution
\rho_{\epsilon}
of (
2.10
) with
\rho_{\epsilon}(\cdot,0)
the following holds. For any
x\in[-N,\dots,N-1]
, any
t\in(0,T]
and any
\epsilon>0

|\rho_{\epsilon}(x,t)-\rho_{\epsilon}(x+1,t)|\leq\min\Big\{1,c\Big(\epsilon\log_{+}(\epsilon^{-2}t)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon^{-2}t}}\Big)\Big\},

(D.1)

where
\log_{+}(u)=\max\{\log(u),1\}
. We shall use a weaker version of (
D.1
), namely that
for any
\zeta>0
and
\tau>0
there is
c
so that

\sup_{x,y\in\Lambda_{N}:|x-y|\leq 1}|\rho_{\epsilon}(x,t)-\rho_{\epsilon}(y,t)|\leq\frac{c}{(\epsilon^{-2}t)^{1/2-\zeta}+1}\quad\text{{\rm for any} $t\leq\tau\log\epsilon^{-1}$}.

(D.2)

Now we note that for our purposes the previous results
are not enough, and we had to obtain more refined estimates as given in Lemma
2.12
whose proof we present below.

Proof of Lemma
2.12
.

The proof of this lemma
shares similar ideas as those of the proof of Lemma 6.2 in
[
10
]
, but given the nature of the equation, some technical details are different.

Fix
x\in\{-N,\dots,N-1\}
and
0\leq t\leq T
. We denote
h_{\epsilon}(x,t)=\rho_{\epsilon}(x,t)-\rho_{t}(\epsilon x)
, where
\rho_{t}
is the solution of the hydrodynamic equation (
2.5
). Then by summing and subtracting appropriate terms, we get

\displaystyle|\rho_{\epsilon}(t,x)-\rho_{\epsilon}(t,x+1)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|h_{\epsilon}(x,t)-h_{\epsilon}(x+1,t)|+|\rho_{t}(\epsilon x)-\rho_{t}(\epsilon(x+1))|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|h_{\epsilon}(x,t)-h_{\epsilon}(x+1,t)|+\epsilon.

Last inequality is obtained from the Mean value theorem and the fact that
\rho_{t}(u)
is a smooth function, see Remark
2.7
.
It remains now to bound the discrete gradient of
h_{\epsilon}(x,t)
. A simple computation shows that
for
x\in\{-N,\dots,N-1\}
,
h_{\epsilon}(x,t)
satisfies

\begin{cases}&\frac{d}{dt}h_{\epsilon}(x,t)=\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{\epsilon}h_{\epsilon}(x,t)+F_{\epsilon}(x,t)+\epsilon^{-1}\frac{j}{2}\Big(\mathbf{1}_{x\in I_{+}}D_{+}\rho_{\epsilon}(x,t)-\mathbf{1}_{x\in I_{-}}D_{-}\rho_{\epsilon}(x,t)\Big)\\
&{h_{\epsilon}(x,0)=0}.\end{cases}

(D.3)

Note that the initial condition is zero since the initial conditions match, i.e.
\rho_{0}\equiv u_{0}
.
Above
F_{\epsilon}(x,t)=\frac{1}{2}\left[\Delta_{\epsilon}\rho_{t}(\epsilon x)-\Delta\rho_{t}(\epsilon x)\right]
. Note that since
\rho_{t}(x)
is smooth then
\|F\|_{\infty}\leq C\epsilon^{2}
. Now, from the Duhamel’s formula, we get

\displaystyle h_{\epsilon}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}d\lambda\sum_{z}P^{(\epsilon)}_{\lambda}(x,z)F_{\epsilon}(z,t-\lambda)+\epsilon^{-1}{\frac{j}{2}}\int_{0}^{t}d\lambda\sum_{z\in I_{+}}P^{(\epsilon)}_{\lambda}(x,z)D_{+}\rho_{\epsilon}(z,t-\lambda)

\displaystyle{-\epsilon^{-1}\frac{j}{2}\int_{0}^{t}d\lambda\sum_{z\in I_{-}}P^{(\epsilon)}_{\lambda}(x,z)D_{-}\rho_{\epsilon}(z,t-\lambda)},

so that

\displaystyle h_{\epsilon}(x,t)-h_{\epsilon}(x+1,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}d\lambda\sum_{z}\left(P^{(\epsilon)}_{\lambda}(x,z)-P^{(\epsilon)}_{\lambda}(x+1,z)\right)F_{\epsilon}(z,t-\lambda)

\displaystyle+\epsilon^{-1}{\frac{j}{2}}\int_{0}^{t}d\lambda\sum_{z\in I_{+}}\left(P^{(\epsilon)}_{\lambda}(x,z)-P^{(\epsilon)}_{\lambda}(x+1,z)\right)D_{+}\rho_{\epsilon}(z,t-\lambda)

\displaystyle-{\epsilon^{-1}{\frac{j}{2}}\int_{0}^{t}d\lambda\sum_{z\in I_{-}}\left(P^{(\epsilon)}_{\lambda}(x,z)-P^{(\epsilon)}_{\lambda}(x+1,z)\right)D_{-}\rho_{\epsilon}(z,t-\lambda)}.

Since
P^{(\epsilon)}_{\lambda}
is a probability and since
\|F\|_{\infty}\leq C\epsilon^{2}
, we can bound the first line on the right hand side of last display by
C\epsilon^{2}t
. Then we obtain

\displaystyle|h_{\epsilon}(x,t)-h_{\epsilon}(x+1,t)|

\displaystyle\leq C\epsilon^{2}t+\epsilon^{-1}{\frac{j}{2}}\int_{0}^{t}d\lambda\sum_{z\in I_{+}}\left|P^{(\epsilon)}_{\lambda}(x,z)-P^{(\epsilon)}_{\lambda}(x+1,z)\right|D_{+}\rho_{\epsilon}(z,t-\lambda)

\displaystyle+\epsilon^{-1}{\frac{j}{2}}\int_{0}^{t}d\lambda\sum_{z\in I_{-}}\left|P^{(\epsilon)}_{\lambda}(x,z)-P^{(\epsilon)}_{\lambda}(x+1,z)\right|D_{-}\rho_{\epsilon}(z,t-\lambda).

From this it follows that

\begin{split}|\rho_{\epsilon}(x,t)-&\rho_{\epsilon}(x+1,tP)|\\
&\leq C(\epsilon^{2}t+\epsilon)+\epsilon^{-1}\frac{j}{2}\int_{0}^{t}d\lambda\sum_{z\in I_{\pm}}\left|P^{(\epsilon)}_{\lambda}(x,z)-P^{(\epsilon)}_{\lambda}(x+1,z)\right|D_{\pm}\rho_{\epsilon}(t-\lambda,z)\\
&\leq C(\epsilon^{2}t+\epsilon)+\epsilon^{-1}\frac{j}{2}\int_{0}^{t}d\lambda\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon^{-2}\lambda}+1}\sum_{z\in I_{\pm}}G_{\epsilon^{-2}\lambda}(x,z)\end{split}

where in the last inequality we have used (
B.12
). Since
\epsilon^{2}t+\epsilon\leq\epsilon(\epsilon T+1)
, we set
c_{T}=\epsilon T+1
and
this finishes the proof.
∎

Corollary D.2
.

Let
x\in\{-N,\dots,N\}
and
0\leq t\leq T
. There exists a constant
C_{T}
which depends on
T
such that for
0\leq\epsilon<1

\displaystyle\sup_{x,y\in\Lambda_{N}:|x-y|\leq 1}|\rho_{\epsilon}(x,t)-\rho_{\epsilon}(y,t)|\leq C_{T}\epsilon^{1-2\zeta}

Proof.

From the proof Lemma
2.12
, we can bound
\sup_{x,y\in\Lambda_{N}:|x-y|\leq 1}|\rho_{\epsilon}(x,t)-\rho_{\epsilon}(y,t)|
by

\displaystyle{\epsilon c_{T}}+\epsilon^{-1}\frac{j}{2}\int_{0}^{t}d\lambda\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon^{-2}\lambda}+1}\sum_{z\in I_{\pm}}G_{\epsilon^{-2}\lambda}(x,z).

Now we treat the integral term above. To that end first we use the estimate in (
B.6
) that as mentioned in Remark
B.1
also holds for the heat kernel, to bound the integral in the last display by a constant times

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-1}\frac{j}{2}\int_{0}^{t}d\lambda\frac{1}{(\sqrt{\epsilon^{-2}\lambda}+1)^{2}}.

Last integral is bounded from above by

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-1}\frac{j}{2}\int_{0}^{t}d\lambda\frac{1}{(\sqrt{\epsilon^{-2}\lambda}+1)^{2(1-\zeta)}}\leq\epsilon^{-1}\frac{j}{2}\int_{0}^{t}d\lambda\frac{1}{(\sqrt{\epsilon^{-2}\lambda})^{2(1-\zeta)}}.

Now by computing the integral explicitly we see that it equals to

\zeta^{-1}\epsilon^{1-2\zeta}t^{\zeta}
.
Putting together all the estimates we see that

\sup_{x,y\in\Lambda_{N}:|x-y|\leq 1}|\rho_{\epsilon}(x,t)-\rho_{\epsilon}(y,t)|\leq C(\epsilon^{2}t+\epsilon+\epsilon^{1-2\zeta}t^{\zeta}).

Then the bound follows since
t\leq T
and
\epsilon<1
.
∎
